Using the controlled model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}---three architectures, five attention ratios, two scales (340M/1.3B), single training runs per configuration---we evaluate 10 benchmarks and decompose per-example effects on four (LAMBADA, HellaSwag, PIQA, ARC-Easy). A proper calibration would require multiple pretraining seeds per architecture within this zoo, which is not available; without this baseline, we cannot bound the seed contribution to our observed cross-architecture disagreement. The within-architecture Jaccard (${\\sim}0.33$) compares models at different attention ratios, not different random seeds; it therefore provides a proxy for, not a direct measurement of, architecture-versus-seed effects.     "newString": "On LAMBADA, aggregate variance decreases with attention while per-example overlap saturates, suggesting architecture-specific mechanisms rather than uniform rescue; this observation is dependent on GDN inclusion and does not replicate on other benchmarks. These findings reveal that aggregate benchmark equivalence between hybrid architectures masks per-example divergence: each recurrent backbone, when augmented with attention, develops architecture-specific response patterns over individual inputs that are invisible to standard evaluation.\n\nFor practitioners",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\nWe present the first systematic per-example analysis of attention effects in hybrid recurrent models. Per-example multiplicity spans a task-dependent variation ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks, with Jaccard uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$) regardless of aggregate convergence. On LAMBADA, aggregate variance decreases with attention while per-example overlap saturates, suggesting architecture-specific mechanisms rather than uniform rescue; this observation is dependent on GDN inclusion and does not replicate on other benchmarks.\n\nFor practitioners selecting among hybrid architectures, these results suggest a concrete protocol. Per-example audits are warranted whenever multiple hybrid designs achieve comparable aggregate scores on a target task: the low Jaccard ($\\leq 0.10$, indicating ${\\sim}90\\%$ non-overlap) means that architectures which appear interchangeable by aggregate metrics affect nearly disjoint sets of examples. In deployment contexts where prediction consistency matters---medical, legal, or safety-critical applications---aggregate equivalence does not imply per-example equivalence, and architecture choice may determine which specific inputs are handled correctly. A proof-of-concept majority vote across three architectures does not outperform the best individual architecture (LAMBADA: $-0.2$\\,pp; ARC-Easy: $-1.1$\\,pp), despite 15--45\\% per-example disagreement. Oracle accuracy---correct if any architecture is correct---reveals substantial complementarity potential ($+7$--$14$\\,pp above best), but majority vote captures only 1--9\\% of this ceiling. The failure of majority vote does not negate the value of per-example information: the oracle gap ($+7$--$14$\\,pp) confirms that complementary knowledge exists across architectures, but accessing it requires selection methods more sophisticated than naive ensembling.\n",

    "newString": "We extend this line of work to a new source of variation, the recurrent backbone itself, showing that architectural differences produce per-example multiplicity that varies systematically by task type under matched training conditions.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{Hybrid Recurrent Architectures.}\nThe design space for combining attention with recurrence spans layer-level interleaving \\citep[Jamba;][]{lieber2024jamba}, local attention pairing \\citep[Griffin, RecurrentGemma;][]{de2024griffin,botev2024recurrentgemma}, matrix-valued recurrence \\citep[RWKV-6;][]{peng2024eagle}, within-head mixing \\citep[Hymba;][]{dong2024hymba}, and unbounded-context hybrid SSMs \\citep[Samba;][]{ren2024samba}. H3 \\citep{fu2023hungry} achieves near-Transformer quality with gated convolutions and SSMs, establishing an early design point in the hybrid space. Mamba-2 \\citep{dao2024mamba2} establishes a duality between structured state spaces and attention; Mamba-3 \\citep{lahoti2026mamba3} extends the design further, while \\citet{ali2025hidden} reveal implicit attention within Mamba. Comparative evaluations confirm that hybrids outperform pure Transformers and SSMs at scale \\citep{waleffe2024empirical}, and that even one attention layer enables content-based routing \\citep{basu2026routing}. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide the controlled model zoo we use, holding training conditions constant while varying architecture and attention ratio.\n\n\\paragraph{Linear Recurrent Models.}\nOur three recurrent backbones represent distinct gating strategies within the linear recurrence family: RetNet \\citep{sun2023retnet} uses multi-scale exponential decay, HGRN2 \\citep{qin2024hgrn2} applies gated state expansion, and GatedDeltaNet \\citep{yang2024gateddeltanet} combines gated updates with delta rules. These descend from S4 \\citep{gu2022s4} and Mamba \\citep{gu2024mamba}, which introduced structured state spaces and input-dependent selection, respectively, and connect to linear attention \\citep{katharopoulos2020transformers} through the state-space--attention duality of Mamba-2.\n\n\\paragraph{Underspecification and Predictive Multiplicity.}\n\\citet{damour2022underspecification} formalized underspecification: equivalent aggregate performance can mask per-prediction divergence. Concrete NLP instances include seed-dependent BERT disagreement \\citep{mccoy2020berts,sellam2022multiberts} and the four-phase convergence pattern of autoregressive LMs \\citep{fehlauer2025convergence}. \\citet{marx2020predictive} introduced predictive multiplicity as a complementary lens, showing that near-optimal models produce conflicting per-example predictions; extensions address probabilistic classification \\citep{watson2023predictive}, Rashomon capacity \\citep{hsu2022rashomon}, reconciliation \\citep{du2025reconciling,haghighat2026resolving}, and cross-model disagreement as a correctness signal \\citep{gorbett2026crossmodel}. \\citet{swayamdipta2020dataset} characterize examples via training dynamics (variability across epochs); our decomposition operates at deployment time across architectures with no training signal. \\citet{black2022model} survey the model multiplicity landscape and \\citet{cooper2024arbitrariness} connect predictive arbitrariness to fairness. These studies attribute divergence to initialization noise or the near-optimal set within a single architecture. We show that architectural variation in hybrid recurrent models produces analogous per-example multiplicity under matched training conditions.\n",

These descend from S4 \\citep{gu2022s4} and Mamba \\citep{gu2024mamba}, which introduced structured state spaces and input-dependent selection, respectively, and connect to linear attention \\citep{katharopoulos2020transformers} through the state-space--attention duality of Mamba-2.",
          " ",

Concrete NLP instances include seed-dependent BERT disagreement \\citep{mccoy2020berts,sellam2022multiberts} and the four-phase convergence pattern of autoregressive LMs \\citep{fehlauer2025convergence}. \\citet{marx2020predictive} introduced predictive multiplicity as a complementary lens, showing that near-optimal models produce conflicting per-example predictions; extensions address probabilistic classification \\citep{watson2023predictive}, Rashomon capacity \\citep{hsu2022rashomon}, reconciliation \\citep{du2025reconciling,haghighat2026resolving}, and cross-model disagreement as a correctness signal \\citep{gorbett2026crossmodel}. \\citet{swayamdipta2020dataset} characterize examples via training dynamics (variability across epochs); our decomposition operates at deployment time across architectures with no training signal. \\citet{black2022model} survey the model multiplicity landscape and \\citet{cooper2024arbitrariness} connect predictive arbitrariness to fairness. These studies attribute divergence to initialization noise or the near-optimal set within a single architecture. We show that architectural variation in hybrid recurrent models produces analogous per-example multiplicity under matched training conditions.",

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
  "old_string": "When attention layers are interleaved into recurrent architectures, they help and hurt almost entirely different examples---pairwise Jaccard overlap among three hybrid designs is at most 0.10, approximately twice the independence baseline. This per-example divergence is invisible in aggregate metrics: on LAMBADA, cross-architecture standard deviation drops 79\\% as attention increases (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$; the heuristic BCa 95\\% CI excludes zero), but each architecture reaches similar accuracy by rescuing and disrupting different inputs. Practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmark scores; architectures that score identically in aggregate may thus carry hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.",
  "new_string": "Hybrid recurrent architectures that appear equivalent by aggregate benchmarks affect nearly disjoint sets of individual examples: adding attention to three controlled linear recurrent backbones produces helped and hurt sets with at most 10\\% pairwise overlap, approximately twice the independence baseline. This per-example divergence is invisible in aggregate metrics: on LAMBADA, cross-architecture standard deviation drops 79\\% as attention increases (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$; the heuristic BCa 95\\% CI excludes zero), but each architecture reaches similar accuracy by rescuing and disrupting different inputs. Practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmark scores. Our results show that architectures scoring identically in aggregate may nonetheless differ on 15--45\\% of individual predictions, meaning the choice of backbone determines which specific inputs a deployed system handles correctly."
}
```

This per-example divergence is invisible in aggregate metrics: on LAMBADA, cross-architecture standard deviation drops 79\\% as attention increases (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$; the heuristic BCa 95\\% CI excludes zero), but each architecture reaches similar accuracy by rescuing and disrupting different inputs. Practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmark scores. Our results show that architectures scoring identically in aggregate may nonetheless differ on 15--45\\% of individual predictions, meaning the choice of backbone determines which specific inputs a deployed system handles correctly.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nWhen attention layers are interleaved into recurrent architectures, they help and hurt almost entirely different examples---pairwise Jaccard overlap among three hybrid designs is at most 0.10, approximately twice the independence baseline. This per-example divergence is invisible in aggregate metrics: on LAMBADA, cross-architecture standard deviation drops 79\\% as attention increases (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$; the heuristic BCa 95\\% CI excludes zero), but each architecture reaches similar accuracy by rescuing and disrupting different inputs. Practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmark scores; architectures that score identically in aggregate may thus carry hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nThis tension arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \\citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \\citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \\citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \\citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.\n\nMost evaluations of hybrid architectures compare aggregate benchmark scores and report that a given attention ratio achieves a certain accuracy averaged across tasks or averaged across architectures. This practice can mask substantial per-example behavioral diversity. \\citet{marx2020predictive} formalized this risk as predictive multiplicity: a prediction problem admits competing models with conflicting predictions on individual inputs. \\citet{damour2022underspecification} documented a related phenomenon, underspecification, showing that models trained from the same pipeline with different random seeds can disagree on individual predictions despite equivalent aggregate performance. \\citet{mccoy2020berts} demonstrated a concrete NLP instance: BERT models trained from different random seeds diverge on specific linguistic constructions. These studies examine variation from initialization or training noise within a single architecture. Whether predictive multiplicity extends to hybrid recurrent architectures, where the source of variation is the recurrent backbone itself, remains unquantified. The question matters because practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmarks; if architectures that score identically in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, such choices carry hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nWe present the first systematic per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios (0\\% to 25\\%) and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters). These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: gated delta rules, multi-scale exponential decay, and gated state expansion. We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed task categories. On four benchmarks---LAMBADA ($N = 5{,}153$), HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$), PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$), and ARC-Easy ($N = 2{,}376$)---we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap using Jaccard similarity. Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central result: aggregate convergence and per-example divergence follow different trajectories as attention ratio increases.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}\n\\caption{Aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled. (a) Cross-architecture standard deviation on LAMBADA decreases gradually with attention ratio at 1.3B (solid) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed). (b) Pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example helped/hurt sets saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat.}\n\\label{fig:decoupling}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOur analysis yields two findings. First, per-example predictive multiplicity varies in magnitude across tasks, forming a task-dependent variation. The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), while pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$). Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs substantially across task types, ranging from 0.14 (reasoning) to 0.30 (retrieval). Attention-driven aggregate convergence is task-specific. Of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA exhibits monotonic reduction in cross-architecture variance ($\\rho = -1.0$, BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0,\\,-0.7]$, heuristic given $n = 3$, \\S\\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}; cross-architecture $\\sigma$ drops 79\\%). LAMBADA \\citep{paperno2016lambada} is a pure context-retrieval task that requires predicting the final word of a passage from long-range dependencies; attention provides direct token-level access to the full context, which offers a natural explanation for why convergence occurs on this task and not others. Benchmarks that involve reasoning or comprehension show no convergence, with all $|\\rho| \\leq 0.7$ and $p \\geq .233$. The Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ does not survive strict correction at $\\alpha = 0.05$; the confidence interval, which excludes zero, provides a complementary characterization of the effect. This convergence is present at 1.3B but absent at 340M (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_convergence}). On LAMBADA, aggregate $\\sigma$ decreases gradually ($0.024 \\to 0.005$) while per-example Jaccard saturates at 4\\% attention (${\\sim}0.09$)---the two quantities are decoupled. The experiments that follow begin with convergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:task_convergence}) because it establishes the aggregate backdrop against which per-example divergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}) and decoupling (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}) are measured.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item We show that architectural variation---under matched training conditions---produces structured per-example predictive multiplicity with a task-dependent variation ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks in three recurrent backbones, with magnitude independent of aggregate convergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}, \\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}, Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}). Unlike seed-driven disagreement within a single architecture \\citep{mccoy2020berts, damour2022underspecification}, this multiplicity arises under matched training conditions where the primary controlled variable is the recurrent backbone, though we cannot fully disentangle architectural from seed-specific effects with single training runs (Limitations).\n    \\item We further observe that on LAMBADA, aggregate $\\sigma$ decreases gradually with attention while pairwise Jaccard saturates at the first attention level, suggesting that attention reduces the performance gap through architecture-specific mechanisms rather than uniform rescue of the same examples (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}). This observation is dependent on GDN inclusion (excluding GDN: $\\rho = -0.7$, $p = .233$) and does not replicate on other benchmarks.\n\\end{itemize}\n",

This observation is dependent on GDN inclusion (excluding GDN: $\\rho = -0.7$, $p = .233$) and does not replicate on other benchmarks.",
    "newString": "    \\item On LAMBADA, we observe that aggregate cross-architecture variance and per-example overlap respond to attention on different timescales: the former decreases gradually while the latter saturates at the first attention level (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}). This suggests that attention narrows aggregate gaps through architecture-specific mechanisms rather than uniform rescue. The aggregate convergence component is dependent on GDN inclusion (\\S\\ref{ssec:robustness}) and does not replicate on other benchmarks.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nHybrid recurrent architectures that appear equivalent by aggregate benchmarks affect nearly disjoint sets of individual examples: adding attention to three controlled linear recurrent backbones produces helped and hurt sets with at most 10\\% pairwise overlap, approximately twice the independence baseline. This per-example divergence is invisible in aggregate metrics: on LAMBADA, cross-architecture standard deviation drops 79\\% as attention increases (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$; the heuristic BCa 95\\% CI excludes zero), but each architecture reaches similar accuracy by rescuing and disrupting different inputs. Practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmark scores. Our results show that architectures scoring identically in aggregate may nonetheless differ on 15--45\\% of individual predictions, meaning the choice of backbone determines which specific inputs a deployed system handles correctly.\n\nThis tension arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \\citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \\citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \\citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \\citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.\n\nMost evaluations of hybrid architectures compare aggregate benchmark scores and report that a given attention ratio achieves a certain accuracy averaged across tasks or averaged across architectures. This practice can mask substantial per-example behavioral diversity. \\citet{marx2020predictive} formalized this risk as predictive multiplicity: a prediction problem admits competing models with conflicting predictions on individual inputs. \\citet{damour2022underspecification} documented a related phenomenon, underspecification, showing that models trained from the same pipeline with different random seeds can disagree on individual predictions despite equivalent aggregate performance. \\citet{mccoy2020berts} demonstrated a concrete NLP instance: BERT models trained from different random seeds diverge on specific linguistic constructions. These studies examine variation from initialization or training noise within a single architecture. Whether predictive multiplicity extends to hybrid recurrent architectures, where the source of variation is the recurrent backbone itself, remains unquantified. The question matters because practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmarks; if architectures that score identically in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, such choices carry hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nWe present the first systematic per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios (0\\% to 25\\%) and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters). These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: gated delta rules, multi-scale exponential decay, and gated state expansion. We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed task categories. On four benchmarks---LAMBADA ($N = 5{,}153$), HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$), PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$), and ARC-Easy ($N = 2{,}376$)---we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap using Jaccard similarity. Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central result: aggregate convergence and per-example divergence follow different trajectories as attention ratio increases.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}\n\\caption{Aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled. (a) Cross-architecture standard deviation on LAMBADA decreases gradually with attention ratio at 1.3B (solid) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed). (b) Pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example helped/hurt sets saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat.}\n\\label{fig:decoupling}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOur analysis yields two findings. First, per-example predictive multiplicity varies in magnitude across tasks, forming a task-dependent variation. The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), while pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$). Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs substantially across task types, ranging from 0.14 (reasoning) to 0.30 (retrieval).\n\nAttention-driven aggregate convergence is task-specific. Of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA exhibits monotonic reduction in cross-architecture variance ($\\rho = -1.0$, BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0,\\,-0.7]$, heuristic given $n = 3$, \\S\\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}; cross-architecture $\\sigma$ drops 79\\%). LAMBADA \\citep{paperno2016lambada} is a pure context-retrieval task that requires predicting the final word of a passage from long-range dependencies; attention provides direct token-level access to the full context, which offers a natural explanation for why convergence occurs on this task and not others. Benchmarks that involve reasoning or comprehension show no convergence, with all $|\\rho| \\leq 0.7$ and $p \\geq .233$. The Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ does not survive strict correction at $\\alpha = 0.05$; the confidence interval, which excludes zero, provides a complementary characterization of the effect. This convergence is present at 1.3B but absent at 340M (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_convergence}). On LAMBADA, aggregate $\\sigma$ decreases gradually ($0.024 \\to 0.005$) while per-example Jaccard saturates at 4\\% attention (${\\sim}0.09$)---the two quantities are decoupled. The experiments that follow begin with convergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:task_convergence}) because it establishes the aggregate backdrop against which per-example divergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}) and decoupling (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}) are measured.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item We show that architectural variation---under matched training conditions---produces structured per-example predictive multiplicity with a task-dependent variation ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks in three recurrent backbones, with magnitude independent of aggregate convergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}, \\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}, Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}). Unlike seed-driven disagreement within a single architecture \\citep{mccoy2020berts, damour2022underspecification}, this multiplicity arises under matched training conditions where the primary controlled variable is the recurrent backbone, though we cannot fully disentangle architectural from seed-specific effects with single training runs (Limitations).\n    \\item We further observe that on LAMBADA, aggregate $\\sigma$ decreases gradually with attention while pairwise Jaccard saturates at the first attention level, suggesting that attention reduces the performance gap through architecture-specific mechanisms rather than uniform rescue of the same examples (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}). This observation is dependent on GDN inclusion (excluding GDN: $\\rho = -0.7$, $p = .233$) and does not replicate on other benchmarks.\n\\end{itemize}\n",

          "-Table~\\ref{tab:convergence} reports cross-architecture $\\sigma$ at each attention ratio for all benchmarks at 1.3B scale. LAMBADA is the only benchmark exhibiting monotonic convergence: $\\rho = -1.0$ ($p = .017$; BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0,\\,-0.7]$, heuristic given $n = 3$ architectures, \\S\\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}; $\\sigma$ drops 79\\%; Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$; excluding GDN: $\\rho = -0.7$, $p = .233$, \\S\\ref{ssec:robustness}). The three recurrent backbones differ in how they compress sequential context---exponential decay (RetNet), gated expansion (HGRN2), and delta rules (GDN)---creating divergent retrieval capacities that attention bypasses via direct token access.           " ",
          " Each architecture displays a distinct profile of per-example changes (Table~\\ref{tab:per_example}). GDN loses a net 153 examples (462 helped, 615 hurt), RetNet loses 69 (426/495), and HGRN2 gains 153 (548/395). HGRN2 is the only architecture for which attention produces a net per-example benefit. Pairwise Jaccard similarity is low: mean $\\bar{J} \\leq 0.104$ for helped, $\\leq 0.096$ for hurt, indicating ${\\sim}90$\\% non-overlap. Overlap significantly exceeds the independence baseline ($J_{\\text{null}} \\approx 0.05$, all $z > 5.9$, $p < .0001$; Appendix~\\ref{app:jaccard_permutation}), but normalizing by the theoretical upper bound $J_{\\max} = \\min(|A|,|B|)/\\max(|A|,|B|)$, observed overlap reaches only 5--7\\% of the achievable range above chance (normalized ES 0.065 helped, 0.047 hurt; per-pair details in Appendix~\\ref{app:normalized_es}), confirming that low Jaccard cannot be attributed to set-size constraints.",
          " ",
          "-No surface-level feature (token count, word frequency, syntactic complexity) reliably separates helped from hurt examples. However, pure-model confidence on the target token is strongly predictive within each architecture: hurt examples have lower target-token probability among pure-correct examples ($d = 0.83$--$0.92$), and helped examples have higher probability among pure-wrong examples ($d = 0.73$--$0.77$); see Appendix~\\ref{app:confidence_margin}. A Kruskal-Wallis test confirms cross-architecture structure in both hurt ($p = .0005$) and helped ($p = .020$) distributions. The confidence margin thus identifies a per-example predictor of which examples will be affected: attention disrupts marginally correct predictions and rescues near-miss errors (we use `rescue' and `disruption' as descriptive labels for observed transitions, not as causal claims about attention mechanisms), but with architecture-specific thresholds that produce the observed cross-architecture divergence. The rescue pattern generalizes: across all three architectures on LAMBADA, HellaSwag, and ARC-Easy, helped examples have significantly higher pure-model target probability than stayed-wrong examples (9/9 tests, $d = 0.13$--$0.60$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cm_cross}). The disruption pattern is weaker on ARC-Easy ($d = 0.13$--$0.26$), absent on HellaSwag under \\texttt{acc\\_norm}, and absent on binary classification (Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq}), likely reflecting structural properties of constrained-choice formats that limit the discriminative power of confidence margins. Full cross-benchmark continuous analysis, including per-architecture effect sizes on multiple-choice formats, appears in Appendix~\\ref{app:cm_cross}. This structured per-example disagreement despite similar aggregate performance constitutes predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} arising under architectural variation \\citep[cf.][]{damour2022underspecification}.",
          "+\\paragraph{Confidence margin mechanism.} No surface-level feature (token count, word frequency, syntactic complexity) reliably separates helped from hurt examples. A Kruskal-Wallis test confirms cross-architecture structure in both hurt ($p = .0005$) and helped ($p = .020$) distributions. The confidence margin thus identifies a per-example predictor of which examples will be affected: attention disrupts marginally correct predictions and rescues near-miss errors (we use `rescue' and `disruption' as descriptive labels for observed transitions, not as causal claims about attention mechanisms), but with architecture-specific thresholds that produce the observed cross-architecture divergence. The rescue pattern generalizes: across all three architectures on LAMBADA, HellaSwag, and ARC-Easy, helped examples have significantly higher pure-model target probability than stayed-wrong examples (9/9 tests, $d = 0.13$--$0.60$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cm_cross}). The disruption pattern is weaker on ARC-Easy ($d = 0.13$--$0.26$), absent on HellaSwag under \\texttt{acc\\_norm}, and absent on binary classification (Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq}), likely reflecting structural properties of constrained-choice formats that limit the discriminative power of confidence margins. Full cross-benchmark continuous analysis, including per-architecture effect sizes on multiple-choice formats, appears in Appendix~\\ref{app:cm_cross}. This structured per-example disagreement despite similar aggregate performance constitutes predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} arising under architectural variation \\citep[cf.][]{damour2022underspecification}.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Multiplicity variation.} Extending the per-example analysis to HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$) and PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$) reveals that predictive multiplicity varies systematically across tasks (Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}). The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), forming a task-dependent variation. Evaluation format does not drive multiplicity: HellaSwag (four-way, $\\alpha = 0.15$) and PIQA (two-choice, $\\alpha = 0.14$) produce nearly identical multiplicity despite different formats; among two-choice benchmarks alone, $\\alpha$ spans $3\\times$ from 0.14 (PIQA) to 0.41 (WinoGrande; Appendix~\\ref{app:winogrande}) to 0.45 (BoolQ; Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq}), covering the full six-benchmark range within a single format. Bootstrap 95\\% CIs confirm the ordering: all adjacent-benchmark CIs are non-overlapping except PIQA and HellaSwag, whose CIs overlap substantially ($[.127,\\,.159]$ vs.\\ $[.141,\\,.155]$; Appendix, Table~\\ref{tab:arceasy_ambiguity}). Moreover, $\\alpha$ does not significantly correlate with task accuracy (Spearman $\\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$). Pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\bar{J} \\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across four benchmarks), indicating that architectures affect different examples regardless of how many examples are affected. Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs $2\\times$ across task types (from reasoning to retrieval), confirming that multiplicity magnitude depends on task, not attention dose \\citep[cf.][]{gorbett2026crossmodel}. The near-identical $\\alpha$ for PIQA (0.14) and HellaSwag (0.15) despite different evaluation formats suggests a shared reasoning-task floor for multiplicity, while retrieval (LAMBADA, 0.30) and mixed (ARC-Easy, 0.21) tasks produce distinctly higher values.",

          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Confidence margin mechanism.} No surface-level feature (token count, word frequency, syntactic complexity) reliably separates helped from hurt examples. However, pure-model confidence on the target token is strongly predictive within each architecture: hurt examples have lower target-token probability among pure-correct examples ($d = 0.83$--$0.92$), and helped examples have higher probability among pure-wrong examples ($d = 0.73$--$0.77$); see Appendix~\\ref{app:confidence_margin}. A Kruskal-Wallis test confirms cross-architecture structure in both hurt ($p = .0005$) and helped ($p = .020$) distributions. The confidence margin thus identifies a per-example predictor of which examples will be affected: attention disrupts marginally correct predictions and rescues near-miss errors (we use `rescue' and `disruption' as descriptive labels for observed transitions, not as causal claims about attention mechanisms), but with architecture-specific thresholds that produce the observed cross-architecture divergence. The rescue pattern generalizes: across all three architectures on LAMBADA, HellaSwag, and ARC-Easy, helped examples have significantly higher pure-model target probability than stayed-wrong examples (9/9 tests, $d = 0.13$--$0.60$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cm_cross}). The disruption pattern is weaker on ARC-Easy ($d = 0.13$--$0.26$), absent on HellaSwag under \\texttt{acc\\_norm}, and absent on binary classification (Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq}), likely reflecting structural properties of constrained-choice formats that limit the discriminative power of confidence margins. Full cross-benchmark continuous analysis, including per-architecture effect sizes on multiple-choice formats, appears in Appendix~\\ref{app:cm_cross}. This structured per-example disagreement despite similar aggregate performance constitutes predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} arising under architectural variation \\citep[cf.][]{damour2022underspecification}.",
          " ",
          "-\\paragraph{Multiplicity variation.} Extending the per-example analysis to HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$) and PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$) reveals that predictive multiplicity varies systematically across tasks (Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}). The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), forming a task-dependent variation. Evaluation format does not drive multiplicity: HellaSwag (four-way, $\\alpha = 0.15$) and PIQA (two-choice, $\\alpha = 0.14$) produce nearly identical multiplicity despite different formats; among two-choice benchmarks alone, $\\alpha$ spans $3\\times$ from 0.14 (PIQA) to 0.41 (WinoGrande; Appendix~\\ref{app:winogrande}) to 0.45 (BoolQ; Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq}), covering the full six-benchmark range within a single format. Bootstrap 95\\% CIs confirm the ordering: all adjacent-benchmark CIs are non-overlapping except PIQA and HellaSwag, whose CIs overlap substantially ($[.127,\\,.159]$ vs.\\ $[.141,\\,.155]$; Appendix, Table~\\ref{tab:arceasy_ambiguity}). Moreover, $\\alpha$ does not significantly correlate with task accuracy (Spearman $\\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$). Pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\bar{J} \\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across four benchmarks), indicating that architectures affect different examples regardless of how many examples are affected. Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs $2\\times$ across task types (from reasoning to retrieval), confirming that multiplicity magnitude depends on task, not attention dose \\citep[cf.][]{gorbett2026crossmodel}. The near-identical $\\alpha$ for PIQA (0.14) and HellaSwag (0.15) despite different evaluation formats suggests a shared reasoning-task floor for multiplicity, while retrieval (LAMBADA, 0.30) and mixed (ARC-Easy, 0.21) tasks produce distinctly higher values.",

The near-identical $\\alpha$ for PIQA (0.14) and HellaSwag (0.15) despite different evaluation formats suggests a shared reasoning-task floor for multiplicity, while retrieval (LAMBADA, 0.30) and mixed (ARC-Easy, 0.21) tasks produce distinctly higher values.",

### User

_source line 4332 · 2026-05-22T07:11:00.055Z_

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   The user is a Director coordinating an EMNLP 2026 ARR submission (deadline May 25, 2026) for "Per-Example Predictive Multiplicity in Hybrid Linear Recurrent Architectures." This session covers three sequential Director instructions:
   
   - **Round 7 completion (3 remaining items)**: C3 Jaccard floor effect text, W2 verification, compile + report
   - **Round 8 review fixes (10 items)**: Merge contributions, delete Equation 1 formalization, various caveats and clarifications across Abstract/Intro/Experiments/Conclusion/Limitations
   - **Writing optimization reflection**: Deep analysis of why the paper scores 3.0 (Findings level), producing a 13-item prioritized improvement plan
   - **Round 9 execution (12 approved items from the optimization plan)**: Narrative Abstract rewrite, opening hook, paragraph splitting, em-dash reduction, hedging reduction, confidence margin elevation, contribution reframing, and more

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **Predictive Multiplicity** (Marx et al. 2020): Competing models with conflicting per-example predictions despite similar aggregate scores
   - **Ambiguity α**: Task-dependent: PIQA 0.14, HellaSwag 0.15, ARC-Easy 0.21, LAMBADA 0.30
   - **Jaccard similarity**: Cross-architecture overlap uniformly low (≤0.10), ~90% non-overlap
   - **Decoupling**: Aggregate σ decreases while per-example Jaccard saturates at first attention level
   - **Ensemble POC**: Majority vote fails (-0.2pp LAMBADA, -1.1pp ARC-Easy); oracle accuracy +7-14pp
   - **Paper Agent role**: Uses `paper_agent_post_to_director` (NOT `main_agent_post_to_director`)
   - **EMNLP 2026**: 8-page main text limit; Limitations/Ethics/References/Appendix unlimited
   - **Em-dash as AI marker**: paper-writing-norms §5.1 identifies `---` overuse as the most visible AI writing signature

3. Files and Code Sections:

   - **`main.tex`** (Abstract, Limitations, Ethics)
     - Abstract FULLY REWRITTEN (P1): Narrative hook opening ("When attention layers are added..."), ensemble failure data moved from Conclusion, preserved "exploratory evidence", "single training runs per configuration", "Bonferroni-adjusted p = .100" per Director constraints
     - Round 8: Added "single training runs per configuration" to setup, deleted 79%, added Limitations caveats (within-arch Jaccard proxy, attention weight aggregate-only)
     - Limitations and Ethics NOT touched in Round 9 (Director constraint: "不要碰 Limitations 和 Ethics Statement")
     - Current Abstract:
     ```
     When attention layers are added to linear recurrent architectures, they help and hurt almost entirely different examples across backbones: pairwise overlap among three controlled hybrid designs is at most 10\%, far below the 78--92\% theoretical maximum. Using the model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid} (three architectures, five attention ratios, two scales, single training runs per configuration), we decompose per-example effects on four benchmarks and find that this predictive multiplicity \citep{marx2020predictive} depends on task type ($\alpha = 0.14$ for reasoning, $0.30$ for retrieval) rather than attention dose. Aggregate convergence is task-specific: only LAMBADA provides exploratory evidence for monotonic variance reduction (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$), and on this benchmark, aggregate variance decreases gradually while per-example overlap saturates at the first attention level. A proof-of-concept majority vote across architectures fails to outperform the best individual model despite 15--45\% per-example disagreement, while oracle accuracy reveals 7--14\,pp of untapped complementarity, suggesting that architectures interchangeable by aggregate metrics may carry hidden consequences for per-example reliability.
     ```

   - **`sections/introduction.tex`**

        "content": "\\section{Experiments}\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe analyze how attention affects cross-architecture performance variance using models from the hybrid recurrent model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}. Our analysis spans three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2), five attention ratios (0\\% pure recurrent, 4\\%, 7.7\\%, 14.3\\%, 25\\%), and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters). In the h$k$-1 notation used throughout, one attention layer is interleaved per every $k{+}1$ layers; h3-1 denotes 25\\% attention. All models within each scale share identical training data and hyperparameters; they differ only in architecture and attention configuration. We evaluate on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed task categories, plus one supplementary benchmark (COPA, $n = 100$).\n\n\\subsection{Task-Specific Convergence}\\label{ssec:task_convergence}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:convergence} reports cross-architecture $\\sigma$ at each attention ratio for all benchmarks at 1.3B scale. LAMBADA is the only benchmark exhibiting monotonic convergence: $\\rho = -1.0$ ($p = .017$; BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0,\\,-0.7]$, heuristic given $n = 3$ architectures, \\S\\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}; $\\sigma$ drops 79\\%; Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$; excluding GDN: $\\rho = -0.7$, $p = .233$, \\S\\ref{ssec:robustness}). This is consistent with attention compensating for differences in how recurrent architectures retrieve from context \\citep{arora2024zoology}---LAMBADA requires predicting a final word from long-range dependencies, and attention provides direct token-level access that narrows architectural differences in recall capacity \\citep{arora2024based}. The three recurrent backbones differ in how they compress sequential context---exponential decay (RetNet), gated expansion (HGRN2), and delta rules (GDN)---creating divergent retrieval capacities that attention bypasses via direct token access. For reasoning and comprehension tasks, the bottleneck lies in multi-step inference rather than context retrieval, so attention cannot similarly eliminate cross-architecture variance. No other benchmark shows significant convergence (all $|\\rho| \\leq 0.7$, $p \\geq .233$; Figure~\\ref{fig:task_specificity}). Full convergence discussion appears in Appendix~\\ref{app:convergence_detail}. Scale analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_convergence}) shows this convergence is present at 1.3B but absent at 340M. Statistical power is inherently limited with $n = 5$ ordinal levels, so this convergence is best interpreted as exploratory.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_task_specificity.pdf}\n\\caption{Spearman $\\rho$ between attention ratio and cross-architecture $\\sigma$ for each benchmark at 1.3B scale. LAMBADA ($\\rho = -1.0$; BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0,\\,-0.7]$, heuristic) is the only benchmark with a negative correlation whose confidence interval excludes zero. Benchmarks are grouped by task category.}\n\\label{fig:task_specificity}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-architecture convergence across benchmarks (1.3B scale). $\\rho$: Spearman correlation between attention ratio and cross-architecture $\\sigma$ across five configurations. $p$: exact permutation test (two-tailed, 120 permutations). $\\sigma$ values reported $\\times 10^{2}$ (accuracy percentage points). Ratio: $\\sigma_{\\text{pure}} / \\sigma_{\\text{h3-1}}$. \\textbf{Bold}: only benchmark with $\\rho = -1.0$.}\n\\label{tab:convergence}\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrr}\n\\toprule\nBenchmark & Category & $\\rho$ & $p$ & $\\sigma_{\\text{pure}}$ & $\\sigma_{\\text{h3-1}}$ & Ratio \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\textbf{LAMBADA$^{\\ddagger}$} & \\textbf{Retrieval} & $\\mathbf{-1.0}$ & \\textbf{.017} & \\textbf{2.41} & \\textbf{0.49} & \\textbf{4.87} \\\\\nBoolQ & Comprehension & $0.2$ & .783 & 1.63 & 2.36 & 0.69 \\\\\nARC-C & Reasoning & $0.6$ & .350 & 0.96 & 1.32 & 0.72 \\\\\nHellaSwag & Reasoning & $0.4$ & .517 & 1.17 & 1.22 & 0.96 \\\\\nPIQA & Reasoning & $-0.5$ & .450 & 0.54 & 0.34 & 1.59 \\\\\nWinoGrande & Reasoning & $0.1$ & .950 & 0.67 & 0.78 & 0.86 \\\\\nARC-E & Mixed & $-0.7$ & .233 & 1.00 & 0.13 & 7.70 \\\\\nMMLU & Mixed & $-0.7$ & .233 & 0.92 & 0.41 & 2.27 \\\\\nSciQ & Mixed & $0.1$ & .950 & 0.87 & 0.74 & 1.19 \\\\\nXStoryCloze & Mixed & $-0.6$ & .350 & 0.82 & 0.55 & 1.49 \\\\\n\\midrule\nCOPA$^{\\dagger}$ & Reasoning & $-0.67$ & .267 & 2.63 & 0.47 & 5.57 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\footnotesize $^{\\dagger}$Supplementary ($n = 100$); excluded from multiple comparison correction.} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\footnotesize $^{\\ddagger}$GDN h3-1 uses 16 attention heads; all other configurations use 32 (\\S\\ref{ssec:robustness}).} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\footnotesize Bonferroni-adjusted $p = 6 \\times 2/120 = 0.100$ (exact); 0.102 arises from rounding raw $p$ to .017 before multiplying.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\subsection{Per-Example Divergence}\\label{ssec:per_example}\n\nDo architectures converge by improving on the same examples? We compare each architecture's pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid on 5{,}153 LAMBADA examples, classifying each as helped (correct only in hybrid), hurt (correct only in pure), or unchanged. Although the decomposition is binary, we verify below that it captures genuine separation in the continuous confidence space (Cohen's $d = 0.73$--$0.92$ between helped and hurt on LAMBADA; cross-benchmark details in Appendix~\\ref{app:cm_cross}). This binarization discards confidence magnitude---a transition from 0.51 to 0.49 is treated identically to one from 0.99 to 0.01---so we supplement it with continuous confidence margin analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:confidence_margin}).\n\nEach architecture displays a distinct profile of per-example changes (Table~\\ref{tab:per_example}). GDN loses a net 153 examples (462 helped, 615 hurt), RetNet loses 69 (426/495), and HGRN2 gains 153 (548/395). HGRN2 is the only architecture for which attention produces a net per-example benefit. Pairwise Jaccard similarity is low: mean $\\bar{J} \\leq 0.104$ for helped, $\\leq 0.096$ for hurt, indicating ${\\sim}90$\\% non-overlap. Overlap significantly exceeds the independence baseline ($J_{\\text{null}} \\approx 0.05$, all $z > 5.9$, $p < .0001$; Appendix~\\ref{app:jaccard_permutation}), but normalizing by the theoretical upper bound $J_{\\max} = \\min(|A|,|B|)/\\max(|A|,|B|)$, observed overlap reaches only 5--7\\% of the achievable range above chance (normalized ES 0.065 helped, 0.047 hurt; per-pair details in Appendix~\\ref{app:normalized_es}), confirming that low Jaccard cannot be attributed to set-size constraints.\n\n\\paragraph{Confidence margin mechanism.} No surface-level feature (token count, word frequency, syntactic complexity) reliably separates helped from hurt examples. However, pure-model confidence on the target token is strongly predictive within each architecture: hurt examples have lower target-token probability among pure-correct examples ($d = 0.83$--$0.92$), and helped examples have higher probability among pure-wrong examples ($d = 0.73$--$0.77$); see Appendix~\\ref{app:confidence_margin}. A Kruskal-Wallis test confirms cross-architecture structure in both hurt ($p = .0005$) and helped ($p = .020$) distributions. The confidence margin thus identifies a per-example predictor of which examples will be affected: attention disrupts marginally correct predictions and rescues near-miss errors (we use `rescue' and `disruption' as descriptive labels for observed transitions, not as causal claims about attention mechanisms), but with architecture-specific thresholds that produce the observed cross-architecture divergence. The rescue pattern generalizes: across all three architectures on LAMBADA, HellaSwag, and ARC-Easy, helped examples have significantly higher pure-model target probability than stayed-wrong examples (9/9 tests, $d = 0.13$--$0.60$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cm_cross}). The disruption pattern is weaker on ARC-Easy ($d = 0.13$--$0.26$), absent on HellaSwag under \\texttt{acc\\_norm}, and absent on binary classification (Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq}), likely reflecting structural properties of constrained-choice formats that limit the discriminative power of confidence margins. Full cross-benchmark continuous analysis, including per-architecture effect sizes on multiple-choice formats, appears in Appendix~\\ref{app:cm_cross}. This structured per-example disagreement despite similar aggregate performance constitutes predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} arising under architectural variation \\citep[cf.][]{damour2022underspecification}.\n\n\\paragraph{Multiplicity variation.} Extending the per-example analysis to HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$) and PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$) reveals that predictive multiplicity varies systematically across tasks (Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}). The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), forming a task-dependent variation. Evaluation format does not drive multiplicity: HellaSwag (four-way, $\\alpha = 0.15$) and PIQA (two-choice, $\\alpha = 0.14$) produce nearly identical multiplicity despite different formats. Bootstrap 95\\% CIs confirm the ordering: all adjacent-benchmark CIs are non-overlapping except PIQA and HellaSwag, whose CIs overlap substantially ($[.127,\\,.159]$ vs.\\ $[.141,\\,.155]$; Appendix, Table~\\ref{tab:arceasy_ambiguity}). Moreover, $\\alpha$ does not significantly correlate with task accuracy (Spearman $\\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$). Pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\bar{J} \\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across four benchmarks), indicating that architectures affect different examples regardless of how many examples are affected. Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs $2\\times$ across task types (from reasoning to retrieval), confirming that multiplicity magnitude depends on task, not attention dose \\citep[cf.][]{gorbett2026crossmodel}. PIQA (0.14) and HellaSwag (0.15) share a reasoning-task floor, while retrieval (LAMBADA, 0.30) and mixed (ARC-Easy, 0.21) tasks produce higher values.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Predictive multiplicity at 1.3B. $\\alpha$: ambiguity at h3-1 \\citep{marx2020predictive}. $\\bar{J}$: mean pairwise Jaccard of helped~(h) and hurt~(u) sets, averaged across four hybrid attention ratios and three architecture pairs. $\\rho_\\alpha$: Spearman $\\rho$ between attention ratio and $\\alpha$. Benchmarks ordered by $\\alpha$.}\n\\label{tab:multiplicity}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n & $N$ & $\\alpha$ & $\\bar{J}_{h}$ & $\\bar{J}_{u}$ & $\\rho_\\alpha$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nPIQA & 1838 & .14 & .091 & .094 & $0.0$ \\\\\nHellaSwag & 10042 & .15 & .095 & .085 & $0.1$ \\\\\nARC-Easy & 2376 & .21 & .092 & .096 & $-0.2$ \\\\\nLAMBADA & 5153 & .30 & .086 & .091 & $-0.6$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\par\\vspace{1pt}\n{\\footnotesize Within-benchmark $\\alpha$ variation $< 0.03$ across ratios. BoolQ, WinoGrande: Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq}, \\ref{app:winogrande}.}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Decoupling}\\label{ssec:decoupling}\n\nA key consequence of this multiplicity is that aggregate convergence and per-example divergence operate on different timescales as attention ratio increases (Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling}).\n\nCross-architecture $\\sigma$ decreases gradually across the five attention ratios: $0.024 \\to 0.016 \\to 0.014 \\to 0.006 \\to 0.005$. In contrast, pairwise Jaccard similarity between architectures' per-example effects saturates at the first attention level (4\\%): approximately 0.095 for helped, with no further increase at higher ratios. Net asymmetry between architectures' per-example profiles increases from 0.013 at 4\\% attention to 0.040 at 25\\%. If convergence arose from attention uniformly rescuing the same difficult examples across architectures, Jaccard would increase in parallel with decreasing $\\sigma$. The observed saturation of Jaccard at the first attention level indicates that each architecture's per-example response to attention is largely idiosyncratic. The observed $\\bar{J} \\approx 0.10$ is far below the theoretical maximum $J_{\\max} \\approx 0.78$--$0.92$ given helped-set sizes, ruling out ceiling constraints: there is ample room for Jaccard to increase if attention were homogenizing per-example behavior (normalized effect size 5--7\\% of achievable range; \\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}).\n\nBootstrap confidence intervals (10{,}000 resamples; heuristic given $n = 3$, \\S\\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}) support this pattern statistically: the 95\\% CI for $\\sigma$ does not overlap between pure $[.019,\\,.030]$ and h3-1 $[.002,\\,.011]$, while the CIs for mean Jaccard overlap completely across all non-zero attention ratios (helped: $[.08,\\,.11]$; hurt: $[.07,\\,.11]$). Within-architecture Jaccard averages ${\\sim}0.33$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:within_arch_stability}), roughly $3\\times$ the cross-architecture value (${\\sim}0.10$). This $3\\times$ gap compares within-architecture variation across attention ratios to cross-architecture variation at matched ratios---a proxy for, not a direct measurement of, seed-versus-architecture effects. Attributing the gap entirely to seed variation would require seeds to exceed the effect of replacing 25\\% of model layers with attention---implausible though not directly testable with the available data.\n\nAttention weight analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:attention_patterns}) shows GDN and RetNet produce nearly identical attention distributions (cosine 0.995) yet differ in per-example effects, suggesting these originate in the recurrent layers. The four-benchmark variation (Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}) confirms independence: LAMBADA ($\\alpha = 0.30$) is the only benchmark showing convergence despite having the highest multiplicity, while PIQA ($\\alpha = 0.14$) and HellaSwag ($\\alpha = 0.15$) show no convergence with the lowest multiplicity. Cross-scale analysis on all four benchmarks reveals that the qualitative pattern of per-example multiplicity is scale-robust: pairwise Jaccard remains stable across scales ($\\bar{J} = 0.08$--$0.10$ at 340M vs.\\ $0.09$--$0.095$ at 1.3B), and architecture-specific helped/hurt profiles persist. While $\\alpha$ is systematically higher at 340M ($\\Delta \\leq 8$\\,pp), this inflation does not correlate with accuracy loss across benchmarks (HellaSwag shows the largest accuracy gap yet one of the smallest $\\alpha$ increases), suggesting capacity-related but not accuracy-driven modulation. At 340M, aggregate convergence is absent on all benchmarks ($\\rho = -0.50$, $p = .45$ for LAMBADA). This dissociation suggests that the \\emph{pattern} of per-example multiplicity---which architectures affect which examples---reflects structural differences in recurrent processing, while absolute $\\alpha$ magnitude is partially modulated by model capacity (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_per_example}).\n\n\\subsection{Robustness}\\label{ssec:robustness}\n\nRemoving GDN weakens LAMBADA convergence from $\\rho = -1.0$ to $\\rho = -0.7$ ($p = .233$), partly because GDN's h3-1 uses 16 attention heads rather than 32 in all other configurations (Appendix~\\ref{app:gdn_confound}). The RetNet--HGRN2 pair preserves the decoupling: all hybrid $\\sigma \\leq 0.008$ while Jaccard saturates at 0.086--0.108. The aggregate convergence result is therefore dependent on GDN inclusion; the decoupling result is not. Full sensitivity analyses appear in Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity}.\n",

  "parentUuid": "9a74da5a-2989-4760-9f39-8c9b64cfb79c",

  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nHybrid recurrent architectures that appear equivalent by aggregate benchmarks affect nearly disjoint sets of individual examples: adding attention to three controlled linear recurrent backbones produces helped and hurt sets with at most 10\\% pairwise overlap, approximately twice the independence baseline. This per-example divergence is invisible in aggregate metrics: on LAMBADA, cross-architecture standard deviation drops 79\\% as attention increases (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$; the heuristic BCa 95\\% CI excludes zero), but each architecture reaches similar accuracy by rescuing and disrupting different inputs. Practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmark scores. Our results show that architectures scoring identically in aggregate may nonetheless differ on 15--45\\% of individual predictions, meaning the choice of backbone determines which specific inputs a deployed system handles correctly.\n\nThis tension arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \\citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \\citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \\citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \\citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.\n\nMost evaluations of hybrid architectures compare aggregate benchmark scores and report that a given attention ratio achieves a certain accuracy averaged across tasks or averaged across architectures. This practice can mask substantial per-example behavioral diversity. \\citet{marx2020predictive} formalized this risk as predictive multiplicity: a prediction problem admits competing models with conflicting predictions on individual inputs. \\citet{damour2022underspecification} documented a related phenomenon, underspecification, showing that models trained from the same pipeline with different random seeds can disagree on individual predictions despite equivalent aggregate performance. \\citet{mccoy2020berts} demonstrated a concrete NLP instance: BERT models trained from different random seeds diverge on specific linguistic constructions. These studies examine variation from initialization or training noise within a single architecture. Whether predictive multiplicity extends to hybrid recurrent architectures, where the source of variation is the recurrent backbone itself, remains unquantified. The question matters because practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmarks; if architectures that score identically in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, such choices carry hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nWe present the first systematic per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios (0\\% to 25\\%) and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters). These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: gated delta rules, multi-scale exponential decay, and gated state expansion. We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed task categories. On four benchmarks---LAMBADA ($N = 5{,}153$), HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$), PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$), and ARC-Easy ($N = 2{,}376$)---we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap using Jaccard similarity. Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central result: aggregate convergence and per-example divergence follow different trajectories as attention ratio increases.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}\n\\caption{Aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled. (a) Cross-architecture standard deviation on LAMBADA decreases gradually with attention ratio at 1.3B (solid) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed). (b) Pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example helped/hurt sets saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat.}\n\\label{fig:decoupling}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOur analysis yields two findings. First, per-example predictive multiplicity varies in magnitude across tasks, forming a task-dependent variation. The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), while pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$). Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs substantially across task types, ranging from 0.14 (reasoning) to 0.30 (retrieval).\n\nAttention-driven aggregate convergence is task-specific. Of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA exhibits monotonic reduction in cross-architecture variance ($\\rho = -1.0$, BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0,\\,-0.7]$, heuristic given $n = 3$, \\S\\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}; cross-architecture $\\sigma$ drops 79\\%). LAMBADA \\citep{paperno2016lambada} is a pure context-retrieval task that requires predicting the final word of a passage from long-range dependencies; attention provides direct token-level access to the full context, which offers a natural explanation for why convergence occurs on this task and not others. Benchmarks that involve reasoning or comprehension show no convergence, with all $|\\rho| \\leq 0.7$ and $p \\geq .233$. The Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ does not survive strict correction at $\\alpha = 0.05$; the confidence interval, which excludes zero, provides a complementary characterization of the effect. This convergence is present at 1.3B but absent at 340M (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_convergence}). On LAMBADA, aggregate $\\sigma$ decreases gradually ($0.024 \\to 0.005$) while per-example Jaccard saturates at 4\\% attention (${\\sim}0.09$)---the two quantities are decoupled. The experiments that follow begin with convergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:task_convergence}) because it establishes the aggregate backdrop against which per-example divergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}) and decoupling (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}) are measured.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item We show that architectural variation---under matched training conditions---produces structured per-example predictive multiplicity with a task-dependent variation ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks in three recurrent backbones, with magnitude independent of aggregate convergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}, \\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}, Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}). Unlike seed-driven disagreement within a single architecture \\citep{mccoy2020berts, damour2022underspecification}, this multiplicity arises under matched training conditions where the primary controlled variable is the recurrent backbone, though we cannot fully disentangle architectural from seed-specific effects with single training runs (Limitations).\n    \\item On LAMBADA, we observe that aggregate cross-architecture variance and per-example overlap respond to attention on different timescales: the former decreases gradually while the latter saturates at the first attention level (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}). This suggests that attention narrows aggregate gaps through architecture-specific mechanisms rather than uniform rescue. The aggregate convergence component is dependent on GDN inclusion (\\S\\ref{ssec:robustness}) and does not replicate on other benchmarks.\n\\end{itemize}\n",
        "numLines": 27,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 27
      }
    },

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/sections/related_work.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{Hybrid Recurrent Architectures.}\nThe design space for combining attention with recurrence spans layer-level interleaving \\citep[Jamba;][]{lieber2024jamba}, local attention pairing \\citep[Griffin, RecurrentGemma;][]{de2024griffin,botev2024recurrentgemma}, matrix-valued recurrence \\citep[RWKV-6;][]{peng2024eagle}, within-head mixing \\citep[Hymba;][]{dong2024hymba}, and unbounded-context hybrid SSMs \\citep[Samba;][]{ren2024samba}. H3 \\citep{fu2023hungry} achieves near-Transformer quality with gated convolutions and SSMs, establishing an early design point in the hybrid space. Mamba-2 \\citep{dao2024mamba2} establishes a duality between structured state spaces and attention; Mamba-3 \\citep{lahoti2026mamba3} extends the design further, while \\citet{ali2025hidden} reveal implicit attention within Mamba. Comparative evaluations confirm that hybrids outperform pure Transformers and SSMs at scale \\citep{waleffe2024empirical}, and that even one attention layer enables content-based routing \\citep{basu2026routing}. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide the controlled model zoo we use, holding training conditions constant while varying architecture and attention ratio.\n\n\\paragraph{Linear Recurrent Models.}\nOur three recurrent backbones represent distinct gating strategies within the linear recurrence family: RetNet \\citep{sun2023retnet} uses multi-scale exponential decay, HGRN2 \\citep{qin2024hgrn2} applies gated state expansion, and GatedDeltaNet \\citep{yang2024gateddeltanet} combines gated updates with delta rules. These descend from S4 \\citep{gu2022s4} and Mamba \\citep{gu2024mamba}, which introduced structured state spaces and input-dependent selection, respectively, and connect to linear attention \\citep{katharopoulos2020transformers} through the state-space--attention duality of Mamba-2.\n\n\\paragraph{Underspecification and Predictive Multiplicity.}\n\\citet{damour2022underspecification} formalized underspecification: equivalent aggregate performance can mask per-prediction divergence. Concrete NLP instances include seed-dependent BERT disagreement \\citep{mccoy2020berts,sellam2022multiberts} and the four-phase convergence pattern of autoregressive LMs \\citep{fehlauer2025convergence}. \\citet{marx2020predictive} introduced predictive multiplicity as a complementary lens, showing that near-optimal models produce conflicting per-example predictions; extensions address probabilistic classification \\citep{watson2023predictive}, Rashomon capacity \\citep{hsu2022rashomon}, reconciliation \\citep{du2025reconciling,haghighat2026resolving}, and cross-model disagreement as a correctness signal \\citep{gorbett2026crossmodel}. \\citet{swayamdipta2020dataset} characterize examples via training dynamics (variability across epochs); our decomposition operates at deployment time across architectures with no training signal. \\citet{black2022model} survey the model multiplicity landscape and \\citet{cooper2024arbitrariness} connect predictive arbitrariness to fairness. These studies attribute divergence to initialization noise or the near-optimal set within a single architecture. We extend this line of work to a new source of variation, the recurrent backbone itself, showing that architectural differences produce per-example multiplicity that varies systematically by task type under matched training conditions.\n",
        "numLines": 11,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 11
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/sections/related_work.tex"

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/sections/conclusion.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\nWe present the first systematic per-example analysis of attention effects in hybrid recurrent models. Per-example multiplicity spans a task-dependent variation ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks, with Jaccard uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$) regardless of aggregate convergence. On LAMBADA, aggregate variance decreases with attention while per-example overlap saturates, suggesting architecture-specific mechanisms rather than uniform rescue; this observation is dependent on GDN inclusion and does not replicate on other benchmarks. These findings reveal that aggregate benchmark equivalence between hybrid architectures masks per-example divergence: each recurrent backbone, when augmented with attention, develops architecture-specific response patterns over individual inputs that are invisible to standard evaluation.\n\nFor practitioners selecting among hybrid architectures, these results suggest a concrete protocol. Per-example audits are warranted whenever multiple hybrid designs achieve comparable aggregate scores on a target task: the low Jaccard ($\\leq 0.10$, indicating ${\\sim}90\\%$ non-overlap) means that architectures which appear interchangeable by aggregate metrics affect nearly disjoint sets of examples. In deployment contexts where prediction consistency matters---medical, legal, or safety-critical applications---aggregate equivalence does not imply per-example equivalence, and architecture choice may determine which specific inputs are handled correctly. A proof-of-concept majority vote across three architectures does not outperform the best individual architecture (LAMBADA: $-0.2$\\,pp; ARC-Easy: $-1.1$\\,pp), despite 15--45\\% per-example disagreement. Oracle accuracy---correct if any architecture is correct---reveals substantial complementarity potential ($+7$--$14$\\,pp above best), but majority vote captures only 1--9\\% of this ceiling. The failure of majority vote does not negate the value of per-example information: the oracle gap ($+7$--$14$\\,pp) confirms that complementary knowledge exists across architectures, but accessing it requires selection methods more sophisticated than naive ensembling.\n",
        "numLines": 6,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 6
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/sections/conclusion.tex"
  },
