{
  "parentUuid": "dfc467c4-097f-4a81-a004-83a96e1d449f",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/main.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/main.tex",
        "content": "\\documentclass[11pt]{article}\n\n\\usepackage[review]{acl}\n\n\\usepackage{times}\n\\usepackage{latexsym}\n\\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}\n\\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}\n\\usepackage{microtype}\n\\usepackage{graphicx}\n\\usepackage{booktabs}\n\\usepackage{amsmath}\n\\usepackage{amssymb}\n\\usepackage{multirow}\n\\usepackage{xcolor}\n\n\\title{Per-Example Predictive Multiplicity in Hybrid Linear Recurrent Architectures}\n\n\\author{Anonymous}\n\n\\begin{document}\n\\maketitle\n\n\\begin{abstract}\nWhen attention layers are added to linear recurrent architectures, they help and hurt almost entirely different examples across backbones: pairwise overlap among three controlled hybrid designs is at most 10\\%, far below the 78--92\\% theoretical maximum. Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid} (three architectures, five attention ratios, two scales, single training runs per configuration), we decompose per-example effects on four benchmarks and find that this predictive multiplicity \\citep{marx2020predictive} depends on task type ($\\alpha = 0.14$ for reasoning, $0.30$ for retrieval) rather than attention dose. Aggregate convergence is task-specific: only LAMBADA provides exploratory evidence for monotonic variance reduction (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$), and on this benchmark, aggregate variance decreases gradually while per-example overlap saturates at the first attention level. A proof-of-concept majority vote across architectures fails to outperform the best individual model despite 15--45\\% per-example disagreement, while oracle accuracy reveals 7--14\\,pp of untapped complementarity, suggesting that architectures interchangeable by aggregate metrics may carry hidden consequences for per-example reliability.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n\\input{sections/introduction}\n\n\\input{sections/related_work}\n\n\\input{sections/method}\n\n\\input{sections/experiments}\n\n\\input{sections/conclusion}\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur analysis has several limitations.\n\n\\paragraph{Coverage.} Per-example analysis covers four main benchmarks; BoolQ and WinoGrande are supplementary (Appendix~\\ref{app:boolq}, \\ref{app:winogrande}). The correlation between $\\alpha$ and task accuracy is moderate but not significant ($\\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$); HellaSwag (60\\%, $\\alpha = 0.15$) and PIQA (73\\%, $\\alpha = 0.14$) show low $\\alpha$ despite varied accuracy, while benchmarks near chance level (BoolQ, WinoGrande) show the highest $\\alpha$. Accuracy alone does not explain the variation, though we cannot fully rule out more complex interactions. We examine three recurrent architectures from one model zoo; the controlled design (shared training data, tokenizer, hyperparameters) isolates architectural effects but limits generalization to other hybrid families.\n\n\\paragraph{Methodological constraints.} With only $n = 5$ attention ratios, the minimum achievable two-tailed $p$-value is approximately 0.017, contributing to the Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ for LAMBADA not reaching conventional significance. Each zoo configuration corresponds to a single training run; we cannot fully disentangle architectural from seed-specific effects, though the $3\\times$ Jaccard gap (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}) provides proxy evidence for architecture as the dominant factor. For context, \\citet{mccoy2020berts} report that BERT models from different fine-tuning seeds disagree on ${\\sim}5$--$7$\\% of MNLI examples; however, their metric (pairwise prediction agreement) differs from ours (Jaccard of transition sets when adding attention), so direct numerical comparison is not meaningful. A proper calibration would require multiple pretraining seeds per architecture within this zoo, which is not available; without this baseline, we cannot bound the seed contribution to our observed cross-architecture disagreement. The within-architecture Jaccard (${\\sim}0.33$) compares models at different attention ratios, not different random seeds; it therefore provides a proxy for, not a direct measurement of, architecture-versus-seed effects. Consequently, all cross-architecture per-example claims are conditioned on single training runs per configuration. At 340M, $\\alpha$ is systematically higher than at 1.3B (by up to 8.4pp on ARC-Easy), but pairwise Jaccard remains stable across scales ($\\bar{J} = 0.08$--$0.10$), indicating that the qualitative pattern of per-example multiplicity is more robust than its absolute magnitude. Our attention weight analysis is descriptive, not causal; it compares aggregate distributions and does not resolve whether per-example attention behavior differs on the specific examples where architectures diverge. We compare only two scale points (340M, 1.3B)---a binary observation, not a scaling law.\n\n\\paragraph{Pre-training only.} All models are evaluated after pre-training without fine-tuning; per-example effects and the decoupling pattern may not persist after task-specific adaptation. We evaluate only discrete accuracy; continuous metrics such as log-probability or calibrated confidence may reveal different patterns of multiplicity.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work analyzes publicly available pretrained language models from the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}. No new data collection, human subjects research, or model training is involved. We identify per-example behavioral differences across architectures, which may inform practitioners about the limitations of aggregate benchmark comparisons but does not introduce new risks.\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\appendix\n\\input{sections/appendix}\n\n\\end{document}\n",
        "numLines": 58,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 58
      }
    },

```text
72	\subsection{Decoupling}\label{ssec:decoupling}
73	
74	A key consequence of this multiplicity is that aggregate convergence and per-example divergence operate on different timescales as attention ratio increases (Figure~\ref{fig:decoupling}).
75	
76	Cross-architecture $\sigma$ decreases gradually across the five attention ratios: $0.024 \to 0.016 \to 0.014 \to 0.006 \to 0.005$. In contrast, pairwise Jaccard similarity between architectures' per-example effects saturates at the first attention level (4\%): approximately 0.095 for helped, with no further increase at higher ratios. Net asymmetry between architectures' per-example profiles increases from 0.013 at 4\% attention to 0.040 at 25\%. If convergence arose from attention uniformly rescuing the same difficult examples across architectures, Jaccard would increase in parallel with decreasing $\sigma$. The observed saturation of Jaccard at the first attention level indicates that each architecture's per-example response to attention is largely idiosyncratic. The observed $\bar{J} \approx 0.10$ is far below the theoretical maximum $J_{\max} \approx 0.78$--$0.92$ given helped-set sizes, ruling out ceiling constraints: there is ample room for Jaccard to increase if attention were homogenizing per-example behavior (normalized effect size 5--7\% of achievable range; \S\ref{ssec:per_example}).
77	
78	Bootstrap confidence intervals (10{,}000 resamples; heuristic given $n = 3$, \S\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}) support this pattern statistically: the 95\% CI for $\sigma$ does not overlap between pure $[.019,\,.030]$ and h3-1 $[.002,\,.011]$, while the CIs for mean Jaccard overlap completely across all non-zero attention ratios (helped: $[.08,\,.11]$; hurt: $[.07,\,.11]$). Within-architecture Jaccard averages ${\sim}0.33$ (Appendix~\ref{app:within_arch_stability}), roughly $3\times$ the cross-architecture value (${\sim}0.10$). This $3\times$ gap compares within-architecture variation across attention ratios to cross-architecture variation at matched ratios---a proxy for, not a direct measurement of, seed-versus-architecture effects. Attributing the gap entirely to seed variation would require seeds to exceed the effect of replacing 25\% of model layers with attention---implausible though not directly testable with the available data.
79	
80	Attention weight analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:attention_patterns}) shows GDN and RetNet produce nearly identical attention distributions (cosine 0.995) yet differ in per-example effects, suggesting these originate in the recurrent layers. The four-benchmark variation (Table~\ref{tab:multiplicity}) confirms independence: LAMBADA ($\alpha = 0.30$) is the only benchmark showing convergence despite having the highest multiplicity, while PIQA ($\alpha = 0.14$) and HellaSwag ($\alpha = 0.15$) show no convergence with the lowest multiplicity. Cross-scale analysis on all four benchmarks reveals that the qualitative pattern of per-example multiplicity is scale-robust: pairwise Jaccard remains stable across scales ($\bar{J} = 0.08$--$0.10$ at 340M vs.\ $0.09$--$0.095$ at 1.3B), and architecture-specific helped/hurt profiles persist. While $\alpha$ is systematically higher at 340M ($\Delta \leq 8$\,pp), this inflation does not correlate with accuracy loss across benchmarks (HellaSwag shows the largest accuracy gap yet one of the smallest $\alpha$ increases), suggesting capacity-related but not accuracy-driven modulation. At 340M, aggregate convergence is absent on all benchmarks ($\rho = -0.50$, $p = .45$ for LAMBADA). This dissociation suggests that the \emph{pattern} of per-example multiplicity---which architectures affect which examples---reflects structural differences in recurrent processing, while absolute $\alpha$ magnitude is partially modulated by model capacity (Appendix~\ref{app:scale_per_example}).
81	
82	\subsection{Robustness}\label{ssec:robustness}
83	
84	Removing GDN weakens LAMBADA convergence from $\rho = -1.0$ to $\rho = -0.7$ ($p = .233$), partly because GDN's h3-1 uses 16 attention heads rather than 32 in all other configurations (Appendix~\ref{app:gdn_confound}). The RetNet--HGRN2 pair preserves the decoupling: all hybrid $\sigma \leq 0.008$ while Jaccard saturates at 0.086--0.108. The aggregate convergence result is therefore dependent on GDN inclusion; the decoupling result is not. Full sensitivity analyses appear in Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity}.
85	
```

_source line 4345 · 2026-05-22T07:11:08.241Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01FJAxPR8GM9rkgLow5m1CiF`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	We present the first systematic per-example analysis of attention effects in hybrid recurrent models. Per-example multiplicity spans a task-dependent variation ($\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks, with Jaccard uniformly low ($\leq 0.10$) regardless of aggregate convergence. On LAMBADA, aggregate variance decreases with attention while per-example overlap saturates, suggesting architecture-specific mechanisms rather than uniform rescue; this observation is dependent on GDN inclusion and does not replicate on other benchmarks. These findings reveal that aggregate benchmark equivalence between hybrid architectures masks per-example divergence: each recurrent backbone, when augmented with attention, develops architecture-specific response patterns over individual inputs that are invisible to standard evaluation.
4	
5	For practitioners selecting among hybrid architectures, these results suggest a concrete protocol. Per-example audits are warranted whenever multiple hybrid designs achieve comparable aggregate scores on a target task: the low Jaccard ($\leq 0.10$, indicating ${\sim}90\%$ non-overlap) means that architectures which appear interchangeable by aggregate metrics affect nearly disjoint sets of examples. In deployment contexts where prediction consistency matters---medical, legal, or safety-critical applications---aggregate equivalence does not imply per-example equivalence, and architecture choice may determine which specific inputs are handled correctly. A proof-of-concept majority vote across three architectures does not outperform the best individual architecture (LAMBADA: $-0.2$\,pp; ARC-Easy: $-1.1$\,pp), despite 15--45\% per-example disagreement. Oracle accuracy---correct if any architecture is correct---reveals substantial complementarity potential ($+7$--$14$\,pp above best), but majority vote captures only 1--9\% of this ceiling. The failure of majority vote does not negate the value of per-example information: the oracle gap ($+7$--$14$\,pp) confirms that complementary knowledge exists across architectures, but accessing it requires selection methods more sophisticated than naive ensembling.
6	
```

</details>

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_018JC1VzRMXYa3rgv16Lg5eV`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	Hybrid recurrent architectures that appear equivalent by aggregate benchmarks affect nearly disjoint sets of individual examples: adding attention to three controlled linear recurrent backbones produces helped and hurt sets with at most 10\% pairwise overlap, approximately twice the independence baseline. This per-example divergence is invisible in aggregate metrics: on LAMBADA, cross-architecture standard deviation drops 79\% as attention increases (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$; the heuristic BCa 95\% CI excludes zero), but each architecture reaches similar accuracy by rescuing and disrupting different inputs. Practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmark scores. Our results show that architectures scoring identically in aggregate may nonetheless differ on 15--45\% of individual predictions, meaning the choice of backbone determines which specific inputs a deployed system handles correctly.
4	
5	This tension arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.
6	
7	Most evaluations of hybrid architectures compare aggregate benchmark scores and report that a given attention ratio achieves a certain accuracy averaged across tasks or averaged across architectures. This practice can mask substantial per-example behavioral diversity. \citet{marx2020predictive} formalized this risk as predictive multiplicity: a prediction problem admits competing models with conflicting predictions on individual inputs. \citet{damour2022underspecification} documented a related phenomenon, underspecification, showing that models trained from the same pipeline with different random seeds can disagree on individual predictions despite equivalent aggregate performance. \citet{mccoy2020berts} demonstrated a concrete NLP instance: BERT models trained from different random seeds diverge on specific linguistic constructions. These studies examine variation from initialization or training noise within a single architecture. Whether predictive multiplicity extends to hybrid recurrent architectures, where the source of variation is the recurrent backbone itself, remains unquantified. The question matters because practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmarks; if architectures that score identically in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, such choices carry hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.
8	
9	We present the first systematic per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios (0\% to 25\%) and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters). These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: gated delta rules, multi-scale exponential decay, and gated state expansion. We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed task categories. On four benchmarks---LAMBADA ($N = 5{,}153$), HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$), PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$), and ARC-Easy ($N = 2{,}376$)---we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap using Jaccard similarity. Figure~\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central result: aggregate convergence and per-example divergence follow different trajectories as attention ratio increases.
10	
11	\begin{figure}[t]
12	\centering
13	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}
14	\caption{Aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled. (a) Cross-architecture standard deviation on LAMBADA decreases gradually with attention ratio at 1.3B (solid) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed). (b) Pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example helped/hurt sets saturates at the first attention level (4\%) and remains flat.}
15	\label{fig:decoupling}
16	\end{figure}
17	
18	Our analysis yields two findings. First, per-example predictive multiplicity varies in magnitude across tasks, forming a task-dependent variation. The ambiguity $\alpha$ \citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), while pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$). Within each benchmark, $\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs substantially across task types, ranging from 0.14 (reasoning) to 0.30 (retrieval).
19	
20	Attention-driven aggregate convergence is task-specific. Of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA exhibits monotonic reduction in cross-architecture variance ($\rho = -1.0$, BCa 95\% CI $[-1.0,\,-0.7]$, heuristic given $n = 3$, \S\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}; cross-architecture $\sigma$ drops 79\%). LAMBADA \citep{paperno2016lambada} is a pure context-retrieval task that requires predicting the final word of a passage from long-range dependencies; attention provides direct token-level access to the full context, which offers a natural explanation for why convergence occurs on this task and not others. Benchmarks that involve reasoning or comprehension show no convergence, with all $|\rho| \leq 0.7$ and $p \geq .233$. The Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ does not survive strict correction at $\alpha = 0.05$; the confidence interval, which excludes zero, provides a complementary characterization of the effect. This convergence is present at 1.3B but absent at 340M (Appendix~\ref{app:scale_convergence}). On LAMBADA, aggregate $\sigma$ decreases gradually ($0.024 \to 0.005$) while per-example Jaccard saturates at 4\% attention (${\sim}0.09$)---the two quantities are decoupled. The experiments that follow begin with convergence (\S\ref{ssec:task_convergence}) because it establishes the aggregate backdrop against which per-example divergence (\S\ref{ssec:per_example}) and decoupling (\S\ref{ssec:decoupling}) are measured.
21	
22	Our contributions are as follows:
23	\begin{itemize}
24	    \item We show that architectural variation---under matched training conditions---produces structured per-example predictive multiplicity with a task-dependent variation ($\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks in three recurrent backbones, with magnitude independent of aggregate convergence (\S\ref{ssec:per_example}, \S\ref{ssec:decoupling}, Table~\ref{tab:multiplicity}). Unlike seed-driven disagreement within a single architecture \citep{mccoy2020berts, damour2022underspecification}, this multiplicity arises under matched training conditions where the primary controlled variable is the recurrent backbone, though we cannot fully disentangle architectural from seed-specific effects with single training runs (Limitations).
25	    \item On LAMBADA, we observe that aggregate cross-architecture variance and per-example overlap respond to attention on different timescales: the former decreases gradually while the latter saturates at the first attention level (\S\ref{ssec:decoupling}). This suggests that attention narrows aggregate gaps through architecture-specific mechanisms rather than uniform rescue. The aggregate convergence component is dependent on GDN inclusion (\S\ref{ssec:robustness}) and does not replicate on other benchmarks.
26	\end{itemize}
27	
```

          "+Bootstrap confidence intervals (10{,}000 resamples; heuristic given $n = 3$, \\S\\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}) support this pattern statistically: the 95\\% CI for $\\sigma$ does not overlap between pure $[.019,\\,.030]$ and h3-1 $[.002,\\,.011]$, while the CIs for mean Jaccard overlap completely across all non-zero attention ratios (helped: $[.08,\\,.11]$; hurt: $[.07,\\,.11]$). Within-architecture Jaccard averages ${\\sim}0.33$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:within_arch_stability}), roughly $3\\times$ the cross-architecture value (${\\sim}0.10$). This $3\\times$ gap compares within-architecture variation across attention ratios to cross-architecture variation at matched ratios, a proxy for, not a direct measurement of, seed-versus-architecture effects.",
          " ",
          " Attention weight analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:attention_patterns}) shows GDN and RetNet produce nearly identical attention distributions (cosine 0.995) yet differ in per-example effects, suggesting these originate in the recurrent layers. The four-benchmark variation (Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}) confirms independence: LAMBADA ($\\alpha = 0.30$) is the only benchmark showing convergence despite having the highest multiplicity, while PIQA ($\\alpha = 0.14$) and HellaSwag ($\\alpha = 0.15$) show no convergence with the lowest multiplicity. Cross-scale analysis on all four benchmarks reveals that the qualitative pattern of per-example multiplicity is scale-robust: pairwise Jaccard remains stable across scales ($\\bar{J} = 0.08$--$0.10$ at 340M vs.\\ $0.09$--$0.095$ at 1.3B), and architecture-specific helped/hurt profiles persist. While $\\alpha$ is systematically higher at 340M ($\\Delta \\leq 8$\\,pp), this inflation does not correlate with accuracy loss across benchmarks (HellaSwag shows the largest accuracy gap yet one of the smallest $\\alpha$ increases), suggesting capacity-related but not accuracy-driven modulation. At 340M, aggregate convergence is absent on all benchmarks ($\\rho = -0.50$, $p = .45$ for LAMBADA). This dissociation suggests that the \\emph{pattern} of per-example multiplicity---which architectures affect which examples---reflects structural differences in recurrent processing, while absolute $\\alpha$ magnitude is partially modulated by model capacity (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_per_example}).",
          " "
        ]
      }
    ],

At 340M, aggregate convergence is absent on all benchmarks ($\\rho = -0.50$, $p = .45$ for LAMBADA). This dissociation suggests that the \\emph{pattern} of per-example multiplicity---which architectures affect which examples---reflects structural differences in recurrent processing, while absolute $\\alpha$ magnitude is partially modulated by model capacity (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_per_example}).",

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "ed8ff04a-bc08-4345-a52e-e513b2316dce",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "3e882744-b0d1-4c95-800a-6ef80d716fcb",
  "type": "user",

  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "On four benchmarks---LAMBADA ($N = 5{,}153$), HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$), PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$), and ARC-Easy ($N = 2{,}376$)---we conduct",
    "newString": "On four benchmarks (LAMBADA, $N = 5{,}153$; HellaSwag, $N = 10{,}042$; PIQA, $N = 1{,}838$; ARC-Easy, $N = 2{,}376$) we conduct",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nHybrid recurrent architectures that appear equivalent by aggregate benchmarks affect nearly disjoint sets of individual examples: adding attention to three controlled linear recurrent backbones produces helped and hurt sets with at most 10\\% pairwise overlap, approximately twice the independence baseline. This per-example divergence is invisible in aggregate metrics: on LAMBADA, cross-architecture standard deviation drops 79\\% as attention increases (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$; the heuristic BCa 95\\% CI excludes zero), but each architecture reaches similar accuracy by rescuing and disrupting different inputs. Practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmark scores. Our results show that architectures scoring identically in aggregate may nonetheless differ on 15--45\\% of individual predictions, meaning the choice of backbone determines which specific inputs a deployed system handles correctly.\n\nThis tension arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \\citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \\citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \\citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \\citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.\n\nMost evaluations of hybrid architectures compare aggregate benchmark scores and report that a given attention ratio achieves a certain accuracy averaged across tasks or averaged across architectures. This practice can mask substantial per-example behavioral diversity. \\citet{marx2020predictive} formalized this risk as predictive multiplicity: a prediction problem admits competing models with conflicting predictions on individual inputs. \\citet{damour2022underspecification} documented a related phenomenon, underspecification, showing that models trained from the same pipeline with different random seeds can disagree on individual predictions despite equivalent aggregate performance. \\citet{mccoy2020berts} demonstrated a concrete NLP instance: BERT models trained from different random seeds diverge on specific linguistic constructions. These studies examine variation from initialization or training noise within a single architecture. Whether predictive multiplicity extends to hybrid recurrent architectures, where the source of variation is the recurrent backbone itself, remains unquantified. The question matters because practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmarks; if architectures that score identically in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, such choices carry hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nWe present the first systematic per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios (0\\% to 25\\%) and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters). These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: gated delta rules, multi-scale exponential decay, and gated state expansion. We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed task categories. On four benchmarks---LAMBADA ($N = 5{,}153$), HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$), PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$), and ARC-Easy ($N = 2{,}376$)---we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap using Jaccard similarity. Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central result: aggregate convergence and per-example divergence follow different trajectories as attention ratio increases.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}\n\\caption{Aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled. (a) Cross-architecture standard deviation on LAMBADA decreases gradually with attention ratio at 1.3B (solid) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed). (b) Pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example helped/hurt sets saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat.}\n\\label{fig:decoupling}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOur analysis yields two findings. First, per-example predictive multiplicity varies in magnitude across tasks, forming a task-dependent variation. The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), while pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$). Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs substantially across task types, ranging from 0.14 (reasoning) to 0.30 (retrieval).\n\nAttention-driven aggregate convergence is task-specific. Of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA exhibits monotonic reduction in cross-architecture variance ($\\rho = -1.0$, BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0,\\,-0.7]$, heuristic given $n = 3$, \\S\\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}; cross-architecture $\\sigma$ drops 79\\%). LAMBADA \\citep{paperno2016lambada} is a pure context-retrieval task that requires predicting the final word of a passage from long-range dependencies; attention provides direct token-level access to the full context, which offers a natural explanation for why convergence occurs on this task and not others. Benchmarks that involve reasoning or comprehension show no convergence, with all $|\\rho| \\leq 0.7$ and $p \\geq .233$. The Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ does not survive strict correction at $\\alpha = 0.05$; the confidence interval, which excludes zero, provides a complementary characterization of the effect. This convergence is present at 1.3B but absent at 340M (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_convergence}). {

    "newString": "(${\\sim}0.09$); the two quantities are decoupled.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nHybrid recurrent architectures that appear equivalent by aggregate benchmarks affect nearly disjoint sets of individual examples: adding attention to three controlled linear recurrent backbones produces helped and hurt sets with at most 10\\% pairwise overlap, approximately twice the independence baseline. This per-example divergence is invisible in aggregate metrics: on LAMBADA, cross-architecture standard deviation drops 79\\% as attention increases (Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$; the heuristic BCa 95\\% CI excludes zero), but each architecture reaches similar accuracy by rescuing and disrupting different inputs. Practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmark scores. Our results show that architectures scoring identically in aggregate may nonetheless differ on 15--45\\% of individual predictions, meaning the choice of backbone determines which specific inputs a deployed system handles correctly.\n\nThis tension arises in the context of a rapid architectural shift toward hybrid recurrent-attention models, which interleave attention layers within linear recurrent backbones. Jamba \\citep{lieber2024jamba}, Griffin \\citep{de2024griffin}, RWKV-6 \\citep{peng2024eagle}, and RecurrentGemma \\citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} each combine recurrence with attention using different mixing strategies. These systems now serve as production-grade alternatives to pure Transformers, and the practical question of how much attention to include is typically answered by comparing aggregate benchmark numbers. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo spanning three recurrent architectures, five attention ratios, and two parameter scales, which enables systematic study of how attention allocation affects these hybrid designs.\n\nMost evaluations of hybrid architectures compare aggregate benchmark scores and report that a given attention ratio achieves a certain accuracy averaged across tasks or averaged across architectures. This practice can mask substantial per-example behavioral diversity. \\citet{marx2020predictive} formalized this risk as predictive multiplicity: a prediction problem admits competing models with conflicting predictions on individual inputs. \\citet{damour2022underspecification} documented a related phenomenon, underspecification, showing that models trained from the same pipeline with different random seeds can disagree on individual predictions despite equivalent aggregate performance. \\citet{mccoy2020berts} demonstrated a concrete NLP instance: BERT models trained from different random seeds diverge on specific linguistic constructions. These studies examine variation from initialization or training noise within a single architecture. Whether predictive multiplicity extends to hybrid recurrent architectures, where the source of variation is the recurrent backbone itself, remains unquantified. The question matters because practitioners increasingly choose between recurrent backbones based on aggregate benchmarks; if architectures that score identically in aggregate behave differently on individual inputs, such choices carry hidden consequences for deployment reliability and ensemble construction.\n\nWe present the first systematic per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. Using the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}, we compare three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios (0\\% to 25\\%) and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters). These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: gated delta rules, multi-scale exponential decay, and gated state expansion. We evaluate all 30 configurations on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed task categories. On four benchmarks (LAMBADA, $N = 5{,}153$; HellaSwag, $N = 10{,}042$; PIQA, $N = 1{,}838$; ARC-Easy, $N = 2{,}376$) we conduct per-example decompositions, classifying each example as helped, hurt, or unchanged when attention is added, and measure cross-architecture overlap using Jaccard similarity. Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central result: aggregate convergence and per-example divergence follow different trajectories as attention ratio increases.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}\n\\caption{Aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled. (a) Cross-architecture standard deviation on LAMBADA decreases gradually with attention ratio at 1.3B (solid) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed). (b) Pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example helped/hurt sets saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat.}\n\\label{fig:decoupling}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOur analysis yields two findings. First, per-example predictive multiplicity varies in magnitude across tasks, forming a task-dependent variation. The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), while pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$). Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs substantially across task types, ranging from 0.14 (reasoning) to 0.30 (retrieval).\n\nAttention-driven aggregate convergence is task-specific. Of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA exhibits monotonic reduction in cross-architecture variance ($\\rho = -1.0$, BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0,\\,-0.7]$, heuristic given $n = 3$, \\S\\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}; cross-architecture $\\sigma$ drops 79\\%). LAMBADA \\citep{paperno2016lambada} is a pure context-retrieval task that requires predicting the final word of a passage from long-range dependencies; attention provides direct token-level access to the full context, which offers a natural explanation for why convergence occurs on this task and not others. Benchmarks that involve reasoning or comprehension show no convergence, with all $|\\rho| \\leq 0.7$ and $p \\geq .233$. The Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ does not survive strict correction at $\\alpha = 0.05$; the confidence interval, which excludes zero, provides a complementary characterization of the effect. This convergence is present at 1.3B but absent at 340M (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_convergence}). On LAMBADA, aggregate $\\sigma$ decreases gradually ($0.024 \\to 0.005$) while per-example Jaccard saturates at 4\\% attention (${\\sim}0.09$)---the two quantities are decoupled. The experiments that follow begin with convergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:task_convergence}) because it establishes the aggregate backdrop against which per-example divergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}) and decoupling (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}) are measured.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item We show that architectural variation---under matched training conditions---produces structured per-example predictive multiplicity with a task-dependent variation ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks in three recurrent backbones, with magnitude independent of aggregate convergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}, \\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}, Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}). Unlike seed-driven disagreement within a single architecture \\citep{mccoy2020berts, damour2022underspecification}, this multiplicity arises under matched training conditions where the primary controlled variable is the recurrent backbone, though we cannot fully disentangle architectural from seed-specific effects with single training runs (Limitations).\n    \\item On LAMBADA, we observe that aggregate cross-architecture variance and per-example overlap respond to attention on different timescales: the former decreases gradually while the latter saturates at the first attention level (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}). This suggests that attention narrows aggregate gaps through architecture-specific mechanisms rather than uniform rescue. The aggregate convergence component is dependent on GDN inclusion (\\S\\ref{ssec:robustness}) and does not replicate on other benchmarks.\n\\end{itemize}\n",

Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling} previews our central result: aggregate convergence and per-example divergence follow different trajectories as attention ratio increases.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_decoupling_hero.pdf}\n\\caption{Aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled. (a) Cross-architecture standard deviation on LAMBADA decreases gradually with attention ratio at 1.3B (solid) but partially rebounds at 340M (dashed). (b) Pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example helped/hurt sets saturates at the first attention level (4\\%) and remains flat.}\n\\label{fig:decoupling}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOur analysis yields two findings. First, per-example predictive multiplicity varies in magnitude across tasks, forming a task-dependent variation. The ambiguity $\\alpha$ \\citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), while pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$). Within each benchmark, $\\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs substantially across task types, ranging from 0.14 (reasoning) to 0.30 (retrieval).\n\nAttention-driven aggregate convergence is task-specific. Of 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA exhibits monotonic reduction in cross-architecture variance ($\\rho = -1.0$, BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0,\\,-0.7]$, heuristic given $n = 3$, \\S\\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}; cross-architecture $\\sigma$ drops 79\\%). LAMBADA \\citep{paperno2016lambada} is a pure context-retrieval task that requires predicting the final word of a passage from long-range dependencies; attention provides direct token-level access to the full context, which offers a natural explanation for why convergence occurs on this task and not others. Benchmarks that involve reasoning or comprehension show no convergence, with all $|\\rho| \\leq 0.7$ and $p \\geq .233$. The Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ does not survive strict correction at $\\alpha = 0.05$; the confidence interval, which excludes zero, provides a complementary characterization of the effect. This convergence is present at 1.3B but absent at 340M (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_convergence}). On LAMBADA, aggregate $\\sigma$ decreases gradually ($0.024 \\to 0.005$) while per-example Jaccard saturates at 4\\% attention (${\\sim}0.09$); the two quantities are decoupled. The experiments that follow begin with convergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:task_convergence}) because it establishes the aggregate backdrop against which per-example divergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}) and decoupling (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}) are measured.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item We show that architectural variation---under matched training conditions---produces structured per-example predictive multiplicity with a task-dependent variation ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks in three recurrent backbones, with magnitude independent of aggregate convergence (\\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}, \\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}, Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}). Unlike seed-driven disagreement within a single architecture \\citep{mccoy2020berts, damour2022underspecification}, this multiplicity arises under matched training conditions where the primary controlled variable is the recurrent backbone, though we cannot fully disentangle architectural from seed-specific effects with single training runs (Limitations).\n    \\item On LAMBADA, we observe that aggregate cross-architecture variance and per-example overlap respond to attention on different timescales: the former decreases gradually while the latter saturates at the first attention level (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}). This suggests that attention narrows aggregate gaps through architecture-specific mechanisms rather than uniform rescue. The aggregate convergence component is dependent on GDN inclusion (\\S\\ref{ssec:robustness}) and does not replicate on other benchmarks.\n\\end{itemize}\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01QyFiQntnTjsZFAkzfBJk79`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Experiments}\label{sec:experiments}
2	
3	We analyze how attention affects cross-architecture performance variance using models from the hybrid recurrent model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid}. Our analysis spans three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2), five attention ratios (0\% pure recurrent, 4\%, 7.7\%, 14.3\%, 25\%), and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters). In the h$k$-1 notation used throughout, one attention layer is interleaved per every $k{+}1$ layers; h3-1 denotes 25\% attention. All models within each scale share identical training data and hyperparameters; they differ only in architecture and attention configuration. We evaluate on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed task categories, plus one supplementary benchmark (COPA, $n = 100$).
4	
5	\subsection{Task-Specific Convergence}\label{ssec:task_convergence}
6	
7	Table~\ref{tab:convergence} reports cross-architecture $\sigma$ at each attention ratio for all benchmarks at 1.3B scale. LAMBADA is the only benchmark exhibiting monotonic convergence: $\rho = -1.0$ ($p = .017$; BCa 95\% CI $[-1.0,\,-0.7]$, heuristic given $n = 3$ architectures, \S\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}; $\sigma$ drops 79\%; Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$; excluding GDN: $\rho = -0.7$, $p = .233$, \S\ref{ssec:robustness}). This is consistent with attention compensating for differences in how recurrent architectures retrieve from context \citep{arora2024zoology}---LAMBADA requires predicting a final word from long-range dependencies, and attention provides direct token-level access that narrows architectural differences in recall capacity \citep{arora2024based}. The three recurrent backbones differ in how they compress sequential context---exponential decay (RetNet), gated expansion (HGRN2), and delta rules (GDN)---creating divergent retrieval capacities that attention bypasses via direct token access. For reasoning and comprehension tasks, the bottleneck lies in multi-step inference rather than context retrieval, so attention cannot similarly eliminate cross-architecture variance. No other benchmark shows significant convergence (all $|\rho| \leq 0.7$, $p \geq .233$; Figure~\ref{fig:task_specificity}). Full convergence discussion appears in Appendix~\ref{app:convergence_detail}. Scale analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:scale_convergence}) shows this convergence is present at 1.3B but absent at 340M. Statistical power is inherently limited with $n = 5$ ordinal levels, so this convergence is best interpreted as exploratory.
8	
9	\begin{figure}[t]
10	\centering
11	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_task_specificity.pdf}
12	\caption{Spearman $\rho$ between attention ratio and cross-architecture $\sigma$ for each benchmark at 1.3B scale. LAMBADA ($\rho = -1.0$; BCa 95\% CI $[-1.0,\,-0.7]$, heuristic) is the only benchmark with a negative correlation whose confidence interval excludes zero. Benchmarks are grouped by task category.}
13	\label{fig:task_specificity}
14	\end{figure}
15	
```

For reasoning and comprehension tasks, the bottleneck lies in multi-step inference rather than context retrieval, so attention cannot similarly eliminate cross-architecture variance. No other benchmark shows significant convergence (all $|\\rho| \\leq 0.7$, $p \\geq .233$; Figure~\\ref{fig:task_specificity}). Full convergence discussion appears in Appendix~\\ref{app:convergence_detail}. Scale analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_convergence}) shows this convergence is present at 1.3B but absent at 340M. Statistical power is inherently limited with $n = 5$ ordinal levels, so this convergence is best interpreted as exploratory.",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01PnPaGS8RHcP1MMdANCePCt`</summary>

```text
43	\subsection{Per-Example Divergence}\label{ssec:per_example}
44	
45	Do architectures converge by improving on the same examples? We compare each architecture's pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid on 5{,}153 LAMBADA examples, classifying each as helped (correct only in hybrid), hurt (correct only in pure), or unchanged. Although the decomposition is binary, we verify below that it captures genuine separation in the continuous confidence space (Cohen's $d = 0.73$--$0.92$ between helped and hurt on LAMBADA; cross-benchmark details in Appendix~\ref{app:cm_cross}). This binarization discards confidence magnitude---a transition from 0.51 to 0.49 is treated identically to one from 0.99 to 0.01---so we supplement it with continuous confidence margin analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:confidence_margin}).
46	
47	Each architecture displays a distinct profile of per-example changes (Table~\ref{tab:per_example}). GDN loses a net 153 examples (462 helped, 615 hurt), RetNet loses 69 (426/495), and HGRN2 gains 153 (548/395). HGRN2 is the only architecture for which attention produces a net per-example benefit. Pairwise Jaccard similarity is low: mean $\bar{J} \leq 0.104$ for helped, $\leq 0.096$ for hurt, indicating ${\sim}90$\% non-overlap. Overlap significantly exceeds the independence baseline ($J_{\text{null}} \approx 0.05$, all $z > 5.9$, $p < .0001$; Appendix~\ref{app:jaccard_permutation}), but normalizing by the theoretical upper bound $J_{\max} = \min(|A|,|B|)/\max(|A|,|B|)$, observed overlap reaches only 5--7\% of the achievable range above chance (normalized ES 0.065 helped, 0.047 hurt; per-pair details in Appendix~\ref{app:normalized_es}), confirming that low Jaccard cannot be attributed to set-size constraints.
48	
49	\paragraph{Confidence margin mechanism.} No surface-level feature (token count, word frequency, syntactic complexity) reliably separates helped from hurt examples. However, pure-model confidence on the target token is strongly predictive within each architecture: hurt examples have lower target-token probability among pure-correct examples ($d = 0.83$--$0.92$), and helped examples have higher probability among pure-wrong examples ($d = 0.73$--$0.77$); see Appendix~\ref{app:confidence_margin}. A Kruskal-Wallis test confirms cross-architecture structure in both hurt ($p = .0005$) and helped ($p = .020$) distributions. The confidence margin thus identifies a per-example predictor of which examples will be affected: attention disrupts marginally correct predictions and rescues near-miss errors (we use `rescue' and `disruption' as descriptive labels for observed transitions, not as causal claims about attention mechanisms), but with architecture-specific thresholds that produce the observed cross-architecture divergence. The rescue pattern generalizes: across all three architectures on LAMBADA, HellaSwag, and ARC-Easy, helped examples have significantly higher pure-model target probability than stayed-wrong examples (9/9 tests, $d = 0.13$--$0.60$; Appendix~\ref{app:cm_cross}). The disruption pattern is weaker on ARC-Easy ($d = 0.13$--$0.26$), absent on HellaSwag under \texttt{acc\_norm}, and absent on binary classification (Appendix~\ref{app:boolq}), likely reflecting structural properties of constrained-choice formats that limit the discriminative power of confidence margins. Full cross-benchmark continuous analysis, including per-architecture effect sizes on multiple-choice formats, appears in Appendix~\ref{app:cm_cross}. This structured per-example disagreement despite similar aggregate performance constitutes predictive multiplicity \citep{marx2020predictive} arising under architectural variation \citep[cf.][]{damour2022underspecification}.
50	
51	\paragraph{Multiplicity variation.} Extending the per-example analysis to HellaSwag ($N = 10{,}042$) and PIQA ($N = 1{,}838$) reveals that predictive multiplicity varies systematically across tasks (Table~\ref{tab:multiplicity}). The ambiguity $\alpha$ \citep{marx2020predictive} ranges from 0.14 (PIQA) through 0.15 (HellaSwag) and 0.21 (ARC-Easy) to 0.30 (LAMBADA), forming a task-dependent variation. Evaluation format does not drive multiplicity: HellaSwag (four-way, $\alpha = 0.15$) and PIQA (two-choice, $\alpha = 0.14$) produce nearly identical multiplicity despite different formats. Bootstrap 95\% CIs confirm the ordering: all adjacent-benchmark CIs are non-overlapping except PIQA and HellaSwag, whose CIs overlap substantially ($[.127,\,.159]$ vs.\ $[.141,\,.155]$; Appendix, Table~\ref{tab:arceasy_ambiguity}). Moreover, $\alpha$ does not significantly correlate with task accuracy (Spearman $\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$). Pairwise Jaccard remains uniformly low ($\bar{J} \leq 0.10$, all $p < .01$ across four benchmarks), indicating that architectures affect different examples regardless of how many examples are affected. Within each benchmark, $\alpha$ is stable across attention ratios (variation $< 0.03$), but differs $2\times$ across task types (from reasoning to retrieval), confirming that multiplicity magnitude depends on task, not attention dose \citep[cf.][]{gorbett2026crossmodel}. PIQA (0.14) and HellaSwag (0.15) share a reasoning-task floor, while retrieval (LAMBADA, 0.30) and mixed (ARC-Easy, 0.21) tasks produce higher values.
52	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_017duPXuhLcfQbfC4i6ZNJYL`</summary>

```text
72	\subsection{Decoupling}\label{ssec:decoupling}
73	
74	A key consequence of this multiplicity is that aggregate convergence and per-example divergence operate on different timescales as attention ratio increases (Figure~\ref{fig:decoupling}).
75	
76	Cross-architecture $\sigma$ decreases gradually across the five attention ratios: $0.024 \to 0.016 \to 0.014 \to 0.006 \to 0.005$. In contrast, pairwise Jaccard similarity between architectures' per-example effects saturates at the first attention level (4\%): approximately 0.095 for helped, with no further increase at higher ratios. Net asymmetry between architectures' per-example profiles increases from 0.013 at 4\% attention to 0.040 at 25\%. If convergence arose from attention uniformly rescuing the same difficult examples across architectures, Jaccard would increase in parallel with decreasing $\sigma$. The observed saturation of Jaccard at the first attention level indicates that each architecture's per-example response to attention is largely idiosyncratic. The observed $\bar{J} \approx 0.10$ is far below the theoretical maximum $J_{\max} \approx 0.78$--$0.92$ given helped-set sizes, ruling out ceiling constraints: there is ample room for Jaccard to increase if attention were homogenizing per-example behavior (normalized effect size 5--7\% of achievable range; \S\ref{ssec:per_example}).
77	
78	Bootstrap confidence intervals (10{,}000 resamples; heuristic given $n = 3$, \S\ref{ssec:statistical_methods}) support this pattern statistically: the 95\% CI for $\sigma$ does not overlap between pure $[.019,\,.030]$ and h3-1 $[.002,\,.011]$, while the CIs for mean Jaccard overlap completely across all non-zero attention ratios (helped: $[.08,\,.11]$; hurt: $[.07,\,.11]$). Within-architecture Jaccard averages ${\sim}0.33$ (Appendix~\ref{app:within_arch_stability}), roughly $3\times$ the cross-architecture value (${\sim}0.10$). This $3\times$ gap compares within-architecture variation across attention ratios to cross-architecture variation at matched ratios, a proxy for, not a direct measurement of, seed-versus-architecture effects.
79	
80	Attention weight analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:attention_patterns}) shows GDN and RetNet produce nearly identical attention distributions (cosine 0.995) yet differ in per-example effects, suggesting these originate in the recurrent layers. The four-benchmark variation (Table~\ref{tab:multiplicity}) confirms independence: LAMBADA ($\alpha = 0.30$) is the only benchmark showing convergence despite having the highest multiplicity, while PIQA ($\alpha = 0.14$) and HellaSwag ($\alpha = 0.15$) show no convergence with the lowest multiplicity.
81	
82	Cross-scale analysis on all four benchmarks reveals that the qualitative pattern of per-example multiplicity is scale-robust: pairwise Jaccard remains stable across scales ($\bar{J} = 0.08$--$0.10$ at 340M vs.\ $0.09$--$0.095$ at 1.3B), and architecture-specific helped/hurt profiles persist. While $\alpha$ is systematically higher at 340M ($\Delta \leq 8$\,pp), this inflation does not correlate with accuracy loss across benchmarks (HellaSwag shows the largest accuracy gap yet one of the smallest $\alpha$ increases), suggesting capacity-related but not accuracy-driven modulation. At 340M, aggregate convergence is absent on all benchmarks ($\rho = -0.50$, $p = .45$ for LAMBADA). This dissociation suggests that the \emph{pattern} of per-example multiplicity---which architectures affect which examples---reflects structural differences in recurrent processing, while absolute $\alpha$ magnitude is partially modulated by model capacity (Appendix~\ref{app:scale_per_example}).
83	
84	\subsection{Robustness}\label{ssec:robustness}
85	
86	Removing GDN weakens LAMBADA convergence from $\rho = -1.0$ to $\rho = -0.7$ ($p = .233$), partly because GDN's h3-1 uses 16 attention heads rather than 32 in all other configurations (Appendix~\ref{app:gdn_confound}). The RetNet--HGRN2 pair preserves the decoupling: all hybrid $\sigma \leq 0.008$ while Jaccard saturates at 0.086--0.108. The aggregate convergence result is therefore dependent on GDN inclusion; the decoupling result is not. Full sensitivity analyses appear in Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity}.
87	
```

          " Attention weight analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:attention_patterns}) shows GDN and RetNet produce nearly identical attention distributions (cosine 0.995) yet differ in per-example effects, suggesting these originate in the recurrent layers. The four-benchmark variation (Table~\\ref{tab:multiplicity}) confirms independence: LAMBADA ($\\alpha = 0.30$) is the only benchmark showing convergence despite having the highest multiplicity, while PIQA ($\\alpha = 0.14$) and HellaSwag ($\\alpha = 0.15$) show no convergence with the lowest multiplicity.",
          " ",
          "-Cross-scale analysis on all four benchmarks reveals that the qualitative pattern of per-example multiplicity is scale-robust: pairwise Jaccard remains stable across scales ($\\bar{J} = 0.08$--$0.10$ at 340M vs.\\ $0.09$--$0.095$ at 1.3B), and architecture-specific helped/hurt profiles persist. While $\\alpha$ is systematically higher at 340M ($\\Delta \\leq 8$\\,pp), this inflation does not correlate with accuracy loss across benchmarks (HellaSwag shows the largest accuracy gap yet one of the smallest $\\alpha$ increases), suggesting capacity-related but not accuracy-driven modulation. At 340M, aggregate convergence is absent on all benchmarks ($\\rho = -0.50$, $p = .45$ for LAMBADA). This dissociation suggests that the \\emph{pattern} of per-example multiplicity---which architectures affect which examples---reflects structural differences in recurrent processing, while absolute $\\alpha$ magnitude is partially modulated by model capacity (Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_per_example}).",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\nWe present the first systematic per-example analysis of attention effects in hybrid recurrent models. Per-example multiplicity spans a task-dependent variation ($\\alpha = 0.14$--$0.30$) across four benchmarks, with Jaccard uniformly low ($\\leq 0.10$) regardless of aggregate convergence. On LAMBADA, aggregate variance decreases with attention while per-example overlap saturates, suggesting architecture-specific mechanisms rather than uniform rescue; this observation is dependent on GDN inclusion and does not replicate on other benchmarks. These findings reveal that aggregate benchmark equivalence between hybrid architectures masks per-example divergence: each recurrent backbone, when augmented with attention, develops architecture-specific response patterns over individual inputs that are invisible to standard evaluation.\n\nFor practitioners selecting among hybrid architectures, these results suggest a concrete protocol. Per-example audits are warranted whenever multiple hybrid designs achieve comparable aggregate scores on a target task: the low Jaccard ($\\leq 0.10$, indicating ${\\sim}90\\%$ non-overlap) means that architectures which appear interchangeable by aggregate metrics affect nearly disjoint sets of examples. In deployment contexts where prediction consistency matters---medical, legal, or safety-critical applications---aggregate equivalence does not imply per-example equivalence, and architecture choice may determine which specific inputs are handled correctly. A proof-of-concept majority vote across three architectures does not outperform the best individual architecture (LAMBADA: $-0.2$\\,pp; ARC-Easy: $-1.1$\\,pp), despite 15--45\\% per-example disagreement. Oracle accuracy---correct if any architecture is correct---reveals substantial complementarity potential ($+7$--$14$\\,pp above best), but majority vote captures only 1--9\\% of this ceiling. The failure of majority vote does not negate the value of per-example information: the oracle gap ($+7$--$14$\\,pp) confirms that complementary knowledge exists across architectures, but accessing it requires selection methods more sophisticated than naive ensembling.\n",

/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/author_response_r7.tex:64:We acknowledge that the within-arch Jaccard (${\sim}0.33$) vs.\ cross-arch Jaccard (${\sim}0.10$) comparison is ``a proxy for, not a direct measurement of, seed-versus-architecture effects'' (\S4.3). We retain it as descriptive because the magnitude is informative: attributing the $3\times$ gap entirely to seed would require seed effects to exceed the impact of replacing 25\% of layers with attention---implausible, though not directly testable.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/author_response_r7.tex:68:\item \textbf{Attention weight similarity}: GDN and RetNet produce nearly identical attention distributions (cosine 0.995) yet diverge substantially in per-example effects (Jaccard $\leq 0.10$). Seed effects would not explain why identical attention produces different outcomes---the divergence must originate in the recurrent layers.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/author_response_r7.tex:99:\item \textbf{Per-example audit}: When hybrid architectures achieve comparable aggregate scores, compute pairwise Jaccard on helped/hurt sets. $\bar{J} \leq 0.10$ (as observed here) indicates ${\sim}90\%$ non-overlap---architectures are affecting nearly disjoint examples.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/author_response_r7.tex:138:Regarding the Jaccard floor effect concern: observed $\bar{J} \approx 0.10$ is far below the theoretical maximum $J_{\max} = 0.78$--$0.92$ (given helped-set sizes of 426--548 out of $N = 5{,}153$). The normalized effect size is only 5--7\% of the achievable range above chance (\S4.2). This rules out ceiling constraints as an alternative explanation for Jaccard saturation---there is ample room for $J$ to increase if attention were homogenizing per-example behavior.}

```text
38	\section*{Limitations}
39	
40	Our analysis has several limitations.
41	
42	\paragraph{Coverage.} Per-example analysis covers four main benchmarks; BoolQ and WinoGrande are supplementary (Appendix~\ref{app:boolq}, \ref{app:winogrande}). The correlation between $\alpha$ and task accuracy is moderate but not significant ($\rho = -0.60$, $p = .24$); HellaSwag (60\%, $\alpha = 0.15$) and PIQA (73\%, $\alpha = 0.14$) show low $\alpha$ despite varied accuracy, while benchmarks near chance level (BoolQ, WinoGrande) show the highest $\alpha$. Accuracy alone does not explain the variation, though we cannot fully rule out more complex interactions. We examine three recurrent architectures from one model zoo; the controlled design (shared training data, tokenizer, hyperparameters) isolates architectural effects but limits generalization to other hybrid families.
43	
44	\paragraph{Methodological constraints.} With only $n = 5$ attention ratios, the minimum achievable two-tailed $p$-value is approximately 0.017, contributing to the Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ for LAMBADA not reaching conventional significance. Each zoo configuration corresponds to a single training run; we cannot fully disentangle architectural from seed-specific effects, though the $3\times$ Jaccard gap (\S\ref{ssec:decoupling}) provides proxy evidence for architecture as the dominant factor. For context, \citet{mccoy2020berts} report that BERT models from different fine-tuning seeds disagree on ${\sim}5$--$7$\% of MNLI examples; however, their metric (pairwise prediction agreement) differs from ours (Jaccard of transition sets when adding attention), so direct numerical comparison is not meaningful. A proper calibration would require multiple pretraining seeds per architecture within this zoo, which is not available; without this baseline, we cannot bound the seed contribution to our observed cross-architecture disagreement. The within-architecture Jaccard (${\sim}0.33$) compares models at different attention ratios, not different random seeds; it therefore provides a proxy for, not a direct measurement of, architecture-versus-seed effects. Consequently, all cross-architecture per-example claims are conditioned on single training runs per configuration. At 340M, $\alpha$ is systematically higher than at 1.3B (by up to 8.4pp on ARC-Easy), but pairwise Jaccard remains stable across scales ($\bar{J} = 0.08$--$0.10$), indicating that the qualitative pattern of per-example multiplicity is more robust than its absolute magnitude. Our attention weight analysis is descriptive, not causal; it compares aggregate distributions and does not resolve whether per-example attention behavior differs on the specific examples where architectures diverge. We compare only two scale points (340M, 1.3B)---a binary observation, not a scaling law.
45	
46	\paragraph{Pre-training only.} All models are evaluated after pre-training without fine-tuning; per-example effects and the decoupling pattern may not persist after task-specific adaptation. We evaluate only discrete accuracy; continuous metrics such as log-probability or calibrated confidence may reveal different patterns of multiplicity.
47	
48	\section*{Ethics Statement}
49	
50	This work analyzes publicly available pretrained language models from the model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid}. No new data collection, human subjects research, or model training is involved. We identify per-example behavioral differences across architectures, which may inform practitioners about the limitations of aggregate benchmark comparisons but does not introduce new risks.
51	
52	\bibliography{references}
53	
54	\appendix
55	\input{sections/appendix}
```

</details>

Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. <details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_019Ls8DFhd1MSSD4kZ7RsQTY`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Method}\label{sec:method}
2	
3	We describe the model configurations, evaluation benchmarks, and analytical framework used to measure how attention affects cross-architecture agreement at both aggregate and per-example levels.
4	
5	\subsection{Model Zoo}\label{ssec:model_zoo}
6	
7	We use the pretrained model zoo of \citet{wang2025hybrid}, which provides controlled comparisons across architectures and attention ratios. The zoo contains three linear recurrent architectures: GatedDeltaNet \citep{yang2024gateddeltanet}, RetNet \citep{sun2023retnet}, and HGRN2 \citep{qin2024hgrn2}. These three architectures represent distinct approaches to linear recurrence: GatedDeltaNet uses gated delta rules, RetNet uses multi-scale exponential decay, and HGRN2 uses gated linear recurrence with state expansion. Each architecture is trained at five attention ratios: 0\% (pure recurrent), 4\%, 7.7\%, 14.3\%, and 25\%. We adopt the h$k$-1 notation from the original work, where one attention layer is interleaved per every $k{+}1$ total layers: h3-1 corresponds to 25\% attention, h6-1 to 14.3\%, h12-1 to 7.7\%, and h24-1 to 4\%. All configurations are available at two scales: 340M and 1.3B parameters. Within each scale, all models share identical training data, tokenizer, and hyperparameters; they differ only in architecture and attention configuration. This controlled design isolates the effect of attention from confounds such as training data or optimization differences. The zoo also includes a Transformer baseline (pure attention) at both scales.
8	
9	\subsection{Task Taxonomy}\label{ssec:task_taxonomy}
10	
11	We evaluate on 10 benchmarks grouped into four categories defined by the primary cognitive demand of each task. This taxonomy was fixed prior to analysis following established distinctions in the hybrid architecture literature \citep{arora2024zoology} (see Appendix~A for the confirmatory/exploratory split), not derived post hoc from observed results. The categories test whether the effect of attention on cross-architecture variance depends on task type.
12	
13	\begin{itemize}
14	    \item \textbf{Retrieval}: LAMBADA \citep{paperno2016lambada}, which requires predicting a final word from long-range context without reasoning or world knowledge.
15	    \item \textbf{Comprehension}: BoolQ, a reading comprehension task with yes/no answers.
16	    \item \textbf{Reasoning}: HellaSwag, ARC-Challenge \citep{clark2018arc}, WinoGrande, and PIQA. COPA ($n = 100$) serves as a supplementary reasoning benchmark; its small sample size renders $\alpha$ estimation unreliable (CI width 0.13--0.18; Appendix~\ref{app:subsample_sensitivity}).
17	    \item \textbf{Mixed}: ARC-Easy, MMLU, SciQ, and XStoryCloze, which combine retrieval, comprehension, and reasoning demands.
18	\end{itemize}
19	
20	All evaluations use lm-evaluation-harness v0.4.x \citep{gao2024lmeval} with default prompts and greedy decoding; accuracy serves as the primary metric for all benchmarks. Bootstrap analyses use random seed 42 throughout.
21	
22	\subsection{Convergence Metrics}\label{ssec:convergence_metrics}
23	
24	We quantify cross-architecture agreement as a function of attention ratio. For each benchmark and attention ratio, we compute the cross-architecture standard deviation $\sigma$ over the three architectures' accuracy scores. To measure the relationship between attention and convergence, we compute the Spearman rank correlation $\rho$ between attention ratio rank and $\sigma$ across the five attention configurations ($n = 5$). A negative $\rho$ indicates that higher attention ratios are associated with lower cross-architecture variance.
25	
26	We also report the convergence ratio $\sigma_{\text{pure}} / \sigma_{\text{h3-1}}$, defined as the standard deviation under the pure-recurrent configuration divided by that under the highest attention ratio (25\%). Values greater than 1 indicate that attention reduces cross-architecture variance.
27	
28	\subsection{Per-Example Decomposition}\label{ssec:per_example_decomposition}
29	
30	To move beyond aggregate metrics, we decompose performance changes at the individual-example level on four benchmarks spanning three task categories: LAMBADA (5{,}153 examples; retrieval), HellaSwag (10{,}042; reasoning), PIQA (1{,}838; reasoning, two-choice), and ARC-Easy (2{,}376; mixed). HellaSwag uses normalized accuracy (acc\_norm) as the per-example correctness criterion. Supplementary analyses on BoolQ (3{,}270; comprehension) and WinoGrande (1{,}267; reasoning, two-choice) appear in Appendix~\ref{app:boolq} and \ref{app:winogrande}. For each architecture, we compare the pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid (25\% attention), which maximizes the contrast between recurrent and hybrid modes; \S\ref{ssec:per_example} confirms that $\alpha$ is stable across intermediate ratios (variation $< 0.03$). We classify each example into one of three categories:
31	
32	\begin{itemize}
33	    \item \textbf{Helped}: correct in the hybrid but incorrect in the pure model.
34	    \item \textbf{Hurt}: correct in the pure model but incorrect in the hybrid.
35	    \item \textbf{Unchanged}: both models agree (both correct or both incorrect).
36	\end{itemize}
37	
38	The net effect for each architecture is $|\text{helped}| - |\text{hurt}|$. A positive net effect means more examples transition to correct than to incorrect when attention is added; a negative net effect means the opposite.
39	
40	To measure cross-architecture overlap in per-example effects, we compute the pairwise Jaccard similarity $J = |A \cap B| / |A \cup B|$ between the helped sets of two architectures and, separately, between their hurt sets. $J = 1$ indicates identical sets; $J = 0$ indicates no overlap. Low $J$ values would indicate that different architectures are affected on different subsets of examples, which would constitute per-example divergence even when aggregate performance converges. On $K$-choice tasks, binarization collapses $K{-}1$ wrong answers into a single ``incorrect'' category, potentially counting architectures as agreeing (both wrong) when they select different wrong answers. This conservative bias strengthens the multiplicity claim: true per-example divergence may exceed what binary Jaccard captures.
41	
42	\subsection{Statistical Methods}\label{ssec:statistical_methods}
43	
44	All statistical tests are two-tailed unless otherwise noted.
45	
46	\paragraph{Permutation test.} With only $n = 5$ attention ratios, we use an exact permutation test for $\rho$. All $5! = 120$ permutations of the attention ratio ranks are enumerated, and the two-tailed $p$-value is the fraction of permutations yielding $|\rho| \geq |\rho_{\text{obs}}|$.
47	
48	\paragraph{Power considerations.} With $n = 5$ ordinal levels, only $\rho = \pm 1.0$ achieves $p < .05$ (exact $p = .017$); the test has no power to detect strong but imperfect monotonic trends ($|\rho| = 0.9$ yields $p = .083$). This binary resolution means that a single rank swap suffices to shift the verdict from significant to non-significant.
49	
50	\paragraph{Bootstrap confidence intervals.} We construct 95\% BCa (bias-corrected and accelerated) bootstrap confidence intervals for $\rho$ using 10{,}000 resamples at the architecture level. With only $n = 3$ architectures, the resampling space is limited (at most 27 unique resamples before considering ratio permutations), so these intervals should be interpreted as heuristic indicators of effect direction rather than intervals with formal coverage guarantees. When the BCa correction degenerates at a boundary value, we report percentile confidence intervals instead. These intervals provide a complementary characterization of effect magnitude alongside the permutation test.
51	
52	\paragraph{Multiple comparisons.} We apply Bonferroni correction across six confirmatory benchmarks (Appendix~A). The four remaining benchmarks are corrected separately across all 10 benchmarks and reported as exploratory.
53	
54	\paragraph{Within- and cross-architecture tests.} For within-architecture comparisons of per-example properties (e.g., token count of helped vs.\ hurt examples), we use the Mann-Whitney U test. For cross-architecture comparisons of per-example effect distributions, we use the Kruskal-Wallis test. Both are rank-based nonparametric tests, chosen because the per-example effect distributions are non-normal and contain ties.
55	
56	\paragraph{Attention weight analysis.} To probe whether attention mechanisms differ between helped and hurt examples, we extract attention weight matrices from each attention layer via forward hooks and compute two quantities: (1) cosine similarity between the mean attention distributions of two architectures at each layer, which measures cross-architecture agreement in attention allocation; and (2) Shannon entropy of the attention distribution for each example, which measures how concentrated or diffuse the attention pattern is. Details of the extraction procedure appear in Appendix~\ref{app:attention_patterns}.
57	
```
